# SolidJS Documentation: full documentation corpus

> Complete Markdown content for the documents listed in llms.txt.

Source index: https://v2-rebuild--solid-docs-v2.netlify.app/llms.txt

# Overview

Source: https://v2-rebuild--solid-docs-v2.netlify.app/index.md

:::note[These docs cover the release candidate]
Solid 2.0 is in its release candidate (RC) phase.
APIs may change before the stable release, and packages from the coordinated RC must use compatible versions.
For Solid 1.x, SolidStart, and the current stable ecosystem, see the [current Solid documentation](https://docs.solidjs.com/).
:::

Solid is a framework for building user interfaces on the web.
Solid 2.0 is a coordinated release of the whole platform: the core library, rendering, routing, head management, and the Vite plugin are designed to work together.

You can try Solid in the [playground](https://playground.solidjs.com/) or [start a project](https://v2-rebuild--solid-docs-v2.netlify.app/getting-started/quick-start) right away.

## How these docs are organized

* [Getting started](https://v2-rebuild--solid-docs-v2.netlify.app/getting-started/quick-start): create a project and learn the project shapes.
* [Concepts](https://v2-rebuild--solid-docs-v2.netlify.app/concepts/reactivity): how Solid works — reactivity, components, and rendering.
* [Building apps](https://v2-rebuild--solid-docs-v2.netlify.app/building-apps/app-structure): the application layer — structure, server functions, sessions, environment, deployment.
* [Routing](https://v2-rebuild--solid-docs-v2.netlify.app/routing/overview): how routers plug in, with guides for Solid Router and TanStack Router.
* [Migration](https://v2-rebuild--solid-docs-v2.netlify.app/migration/from-solid-1): moving from Solid 1.x, SolidStart, and earlier ecosystem versions.
* [Reference](https://v2-rebuild--solid-docs-v2.netlify.app/reference/solid-js/reactivity/create-effect): API documentation for each package, organized by import specifier.
---

# Quick start

Source: https://v2-rebuild--solid-docs-v2.netlify.app/getting-started/quick-start.md

Install Node.js `20.19` or later, or `22.12` or later.
These versions satisfy the Vite 8 runtime requirement used by the maintained templates.

Run the Solid CLI:

```bash
npm init solid@latest
```

Use the equivalent command for another package manager:

```bash
pnpm create solid@latest
yarn create solid@latest
bun create solid@latest
```

Choose the `basic` project shape when the CLI prompts you.
Choose a project directory and package manager, then run the development command printed by the CLI.

Open the local URL printed by the development server to see your app.

The `basic` project includes:

* A router with file-system routes.
* Per-page titles.
* A test suite.
* A purely static production build.

The source template has no hand-written `index.html` or mount file.
The Vite plugin's start mode generates the entries around two files:

* `src/App.tsx` is the app, router included.
* `src/Document.tsx` is the document shell. Site-wide head tags go here.

The static production build emits `dist/client/index.html`.

Edit files under `src/routes` and the route table follows.

## Other project shapes

The CLI offers three project shapes, each a superset of the last.
Pick the one that matches what you are building, or read about [project shapes](https://v2-rebuild--solid-docs-v2.netlify.app/getting-started/project-shapes) to compare them.

* `bare`: no router, pure static output.
* `basic`: router, file-system routes, testing. Still pure static.
* `fullstack`: streaming SSR with server functions, sessions, and API routes.

## Getting help

If you need assistance, ask in the [Discord chatroom](https://discord.com/invite/solidjs).
---

# Project shapes

Source: https://v2-rebuild--solid-docs-v2.netlify.app/getting-started/project-shapes.md

The Solid CLI offers three project shapes.
Each tier is a strict superset of the last and defines its deployment output.

| Tier        | Adds                                                  | Deployment contract                                          |
| ----------- | ----------------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------ |
| `bare`      | Solid, nothing else                                   | `vite build` emits a purely static site                      |
| `basic`     | Router, file-system routes, per-page titles, testing  | Still purely static; deploy `dist/client` to any static host |
| `fullstack` | Streaming SSR, server functions, sessions, API routes | Static client assets plus a request handler in `dist/server` |

Start with the smallest tier that does the job.
Moving up a tier later does not change the structure of your app: the same `src/App.tsx` and `src/Document.tsx` conventions carry through all three.

## Enable server-side rendering

Every tier can enable server rendering with one boolean in `vite.config.ts`:

```ts
import { defineConfig } from "vite";
import solid from "@solidjs/vite-plugin";

export default defineConfig({
	plugins: [
		solid({
			start: true,
			ssr: true, // remove for a static shell rendered on the client
		}),
	],
});
```

The `src/App.tsx` and `src/Document.tsx` structure remains the same.
Application code must still be safe to evaluate and render on the server.
Move browser-only APIs behind client lifecycle code or a client-only boundary, and make the initial server and client output deterministic.
In client mode the document shell is prerendered as static HTML and pages render in the browser; with `ssr: true` pages stream from the server and hydrate.
See [server and client boundaries](https://v2-rebuild--solid-docs-v2.netlify.app/concepts/rendering-and-ssr#server-and-client-boundaries).

## Deploying `fullstack`

The built server entry exports `handleRequest(request)`, a fetch-compatible `Request -> Promise<Response>` handler:

```js
import { handleRequest } from "./dist/server/server.js";

// serve dist/client statically; everything else:
const response = await handleRequest(request);
```

The template's included `server.js` is the Node version of exactly that.
On a fetch-native platform, map `handleRequest` to the host's request entry point and configure its static asset service for `dist/client`.
Workers, Deno, and Bun have separate module, asset, and environment configuration requirements.
See [Deployment](https://v2-rebuild--solid-docs-v2.netlify.app/building-apps/deployment) for platform specifics.
---

# Reactivity

Source: https://v2-rebuild--solid-docs-v2.netlify.app/concepts/reactivity.md

Solid connects reactive values to the computations that read them.
A computation records its dependencies while it runs, and Solid schedules it again when one of those dependencies changes.
This read-based model applies to JSX expressions, memos, and the compute phase of effects.

## A practical mental model

1. A signal or store property is a source of reactive state.
2. Reading a source inside a tracking scope subscribes that scope to the source.
3. Writing to a source stages a new value and notifies its subscribers.
4. Solid drains the pending work as one update pass on the microtask queue.

Dependencies follow the reads taken during the latest run.
If a computation takes a different branch, reads from the old branch stop being dependencies and reads from the new branch become dependencies.

## Signals

A signal provides an accessor for reading a value and a setter for changing it.
Calling the accessor inside a tracking scope creates a dependency, while calling it outside a tracking scope returns the current value without creating a subscription.

```tsx
import { createSignal } from "solid-js";

export function Counter() {
	const [count, setCount] = createSignal(0);
	const doubled = () => count() * 2;

	return (
		<button type="button" onClick={() => setCount((value) => value + 1)}>
			{count()} × 2 = {doubled()}
		</button>
	);
}
```

The JSX expressions create tracking scopes.
When JSX calls `doubled()`, its read of `count()` becomes a dependency of that JSX consumer.
The updater passed to `setCount` receives the latest staged value, so updater functions compose when several writes happen before a flush.

Reactive reads belong in JSX or another tracking scope rather than at the top level of a component body.
Component bodies run untracked, so a top-level read is a one-time snapshot and produces a development warning.

See the [`createSignal` reference](https://v2-rebuild--solid-docs-v2.netlify.app/reference/solid-js/reactivity/create-signal) for setter forms and equality options.

## Memos

Not every derived value needs a memo.
The `doubled` function above runs in its caller's tracking scope and recalculates each time the caller invokes it.

A memo is a read-only derived value.
Its compute function tracks the reactive values it reads, and the memo caches its result for downstream readers.
When the result changes according to the memo's equality rule, Solid notifies the computations that read the memo.

```ts
import { createMemo, createRoot, createSignal, flush } from "solid-js";

const model = createRoot((dispose) => {
	const [first, setFirst] = createSignal("Ada");
	const [last] = createSignal("Lovelace");
	const fullName = createMemo(() => `${first()} ${last()}`);

	return { fullName, setFirst, dispose };
});

console.log(model.fullName()); // "Ada Lovelace"

model.setFirst("Grace");
console.log(model.fullName()); // "Ada Lovelace"

flush();
console.log(model.fullName()); // "Grace Lovelace"

model.dispose();
```

:::note
`model.fullName()` doesn't update until you call `flush()`.
:::

Use a memo when multiple consumers need the same derivation or when the derived value should form an equality boundary in the reactive graph.
An expression used in only one JSX location can often read its sources directly.

Async memos normally use the readiness and loading behavior described on the [async reactivity page](https://v2-rebuild--solid-docs-v2.netlify.app/concepts/async-reactivity).
That page also covers the advanced `loadingValue` escape hatch.

## Effects

An effect connects reactive computation to imperative work.
In Solid 2.0, `createEffect` has a tracked compute phase and an untracked effect phase.
The value returned by the compute function is passed to the effect function after the queue flushes.
Use an effect to send reactive output to code outside the reactive graph, such as a logger, DOM API, or subscription.
Use a plain derived function or memo when other reactive code needs the result.
See [Avoid unnecessary effects](https://v2-rebuild--solid-docs-v2.netlify.app/guides/avoid-unnecessary-effects) for synchronous and async derivation, writable derived state, and interaction-driven work.

```ts
import { createEffect, createRoot, createSignal, flush } from "solid-js";

const counter = createRoot((dispose) => {
	const [count, setCount] = createSignal(0);

	createEffect(
		() => count(),
		(value, previous) => console.log({ previous, value })
	);

	return { setCount, dispose };
});

flush(); // logs { previous: undefined, value: 0 }

counter.setCount((value) => value + 1);
flush(); // logs { previous: 0, value: 1 }

counter.dispose();
```

Put every reactive read that should retrigger the effect in the compute function.
Reads in the effect function do not become dependencies.
The effect function may return a cleanup function, which runs before the next effect invocation and when the effect is disposed.

See the [`createEffect` reference](https://v2-rebuild--solid-docs-v2.netlify.app/reference/solid-js/reactivity/create-effect) for cleanup, scheduling, and error handling.

## Update scheduling

Signal and store writes are batched by default.
A setter stages its value, and ordinary reads continue to return the committed value until Solid drains the queue in a microtask.
Several writes in the same turn therefore update downstream computations together.

Most application code can rely on this scheduled flush.
Use [`flush()`](https://v2-rebuild--solid-docs-v2.netlify.app/reference/solid-js/reactivity/flush) when a test or imperative integration must observe pending writes synchronously.

## Ownership and disposal

Reactive computations are organized under owners.
Memos, effects, and cleanups created while an owner is current attach to that owner, and disposing the owner removes its child computations and subscriptions.
Children created by a computation are also disposed before that computation runs again.

Components provide an owner for the reactive work created during their lifetime.
Use [`createRoot`](https://v2-rebuild--solid-docs-v2.netlify.app/reference/solid-js/advanced/owner-introspection/create-root) to host a reactive scope outside a component, and call its disposer when that scope should end.
Creating an effect without an owner leaves no lifecycle that can dispose it.

Async computations, loading state, actions, and refresh behavior are covered on the [async reactivity page](https://v2-rebuild--solid-docs-v2.netlify.app/concepts/async-reactivity).
---

# Stores

Source: https://v2-rebuild--solid-docs-v2.netlify.app/concepts/stores.md

A store represents object or array state through a reactive proxy.
Store properties use property syntax rather than accessors, and nested objects and arrays are exposed through reactive proxies.
Each property read is tracked, including reads from nested objects and arrays.
Updates use a setter that exposes a mutable draft while keeping the store itself read-only.

Use a signal when a value is read and replaced as one unit.
Use a store when consumers need to read or update separate parts of a nested object or collection.

## Create nested state

`createStore` returns the store proxy and its setter.
Reading `state.profile.name` in a tracking scope subscribes that scope to the nested `name` property.
A write to another property does not notify that subscription.
Solid creates property tracking nodes lazily as tracked consumers read each property.
The proxy keeps one nested object interface, so individual fields do not need accessor wrappers.

```ts
import { createEffect, createRoot, createStore, flush } from "solid-js";

type Todo = {
	id: string;
	text: string;
	done: boolean;
};

const model = createRoot((dispose) => {
	const [state, setState] = createStore({
		profile: { name: "Ada", online: false },
		todos: [] as Todo[],
	});

	createEffect(
		() => state.profile.name,
		(name) => console.log(`Name: ${name}`)
	);

	return { state, setState, dispose };
});

flush(); // logs "Name: Ada"

model.setState((draft) => {
	draft.profile.name = "Grace";
	draft.todos.push({ id: "1", text: "Write docs", done: false });
});

flush(); // logs "Name: Grace"
model.dispose();
```

Store properties are values rather than accessors, so read `state.profile.name` without calling it.
Keep the property access inside JSX, a memo, or an effect compute function when future writes should update the consumer.

See the [`createStore` reference](https://v2-rebuild--solid-docs-v2.netlify.app/reference/solid-js/stores/create-store) for options and return types.

## Update with a draft

The store setter receives a draft that can be changed with normal property assignments and array methods.
This callback is the produce-style update API for stores in Solid 2.0.

```ts
model.setState((draft) => {
	draft.profile.online = true;

	const todo = draft.todos.find((todo) => todo.id === "1");
	if (todo) todo.done = true;
});
```

The callback may instead return a replacement value.
For arrays, Solid writes the returned entries by index and adjusts the length.
For objects, Solid shallowly writes present keys and deletes keys missing from the returned object.

```ts
const [todos, setTodos] = createStore<Todo[]>([
	{ id: "1", text: "Write docs", done: true },
	{ id: "2", text: "Review examples", done: false },
]);

setTodos((current) => current.filter((todo) => !todo.done));
```

Returning a collection does not perform keyed reconciliation.
Use a projection when items that survive a replacement need to retain their store identity.

[`storePath`](https://v2-rebuild--solid-docs-v2.netlify.app/reference/solid-js/advanced/store-advanced/store-path) remains available as an optional path-based helper.
The draft callback is the primary setter form.

```ts
import { storePath } from "solid-js";

model.setState(storePath("profile", "name", "Katherine"));
```

Store writes use the same microtask batching as signal writes, so ordinary reads see the committed state after the scheduled flush.

## Derive a store with a projection

A projection is derived state with store-shaped, per-property tracking.
`createProjection` returns a read-only store, while the function form of `createStore` returns a projected store with a setter.
For a derived scalar or another value consumed as one unit, use a memo instead.

The projection function receives a draft and may mutate it or return a replacement.
Returned collections are reconciled by `id` by default, which lets surviving items keep their proxy identity.
Pass another key when the data uses a different identity field, or `null` for positional reconciliation.

The required seed is the backing object or array for the projected store.
Each result reconciles into the same projected store, so the root proxy keeps its identity.
For an async projection, the seed is not a committed first result by default.
First-flight reads use the readiness and loading behavior described on the [async reactivity page](https://v2-rebuild--solid-docs-v2.netlify.app/concepts/async-reactivity).
That page also covers the advanced `seedLoadingValue` escape hatch.

```ts
import {
	createProjection,
	createRoot,
	createSignal,
	createStore,
	flush,
} from "solid-js";

type Todo = {
	id: string;
	text: string;
	done: boolean;
};

const list = createRoot((dispose) => {
	const [todos] = createStore<Todo[]>([
		{ id: "1", text: "Write docs", done: true },
		{ id: "2", text: "Review examples", done: false },
	]);
	const [includeDone, setIncludeDone] = createSignal(false);
	const visible = createProjection(
		() => todos.filter((todo) => includeDone() || !todo.done),
		[]
	);

	return { visible, setIncludeDone, dispose };
});

console.log(list.visible.map((todo) => todo.text)); // ["Review examples"]

list.setIncludeDone(true);
flush();
console.log(list.visible.map((todo) => todo.text)); // ["Write docs", "Review examples"]

list.dispose();
```

See the [`createProjection` reference](https://v2-rebuild--solid-docs-v2.netlify.app/reference/solid-js/stores/create-projection) for mutation and return forms.

## Optimistic stores

`createOptimisticStore` has the same nested draft update model with a tentative overlay.
A tentative write is visible immediately.
When the action settles, Solid removes the overlay and reveals the authoritative store value.

```ts
import { action, createOptimisticStore } from "solid-js";

const [profile, setProfile] = createOptimisticStore({ name: "Ada" });

const rename = action(function* (name: string) {
	setProfile((draft) => {
		draft.name = name;
	});

	console.log(profile.name); // "Grace" during rename("Grace")
	yield Promise.resolve();
});

await rename("Grace");
console.log(profile.name); // "Ada"
```

The derived form recomputes an authoritative value through a projection.
When fresh projected data lands, it updates the base state and clears stale optimistic overlays.
This form is useful when an action shows an expected collection update before refreshing its source.

See the [`createOptimisticStore` reference](https://v2-rebuild--solid-docs-v2.netlify.app/reference/solid-js/stores/create-optimistic-store) for both forms, and see [async reactivity](https://v2-rebuild--solid-docs-v2.netlify.app/concepts/async-reactivity) for actions and refresh behavior.
---

# Components and JSX

Source: https://v2-rebuild--solid-docs-v2.netlify.app/concepts/components-and-jsx.md

Components are reusable units that combine rendering structure with related behavior.
They nest to form a component tree, from the application root down to individual controls.
A component is a function that receives props and returns elements.

## How JSX executes

JSX keeps element structure and JavaScript expressions in the same source.
Use curly braces to place an expression inside an element.
Lowercase JSX names describe native elements, while names that begin with a capital letter refer to components.

Solid compiles JSX into renderer operations.
In a browser build, those operations create or claim DOM nodes and connect reactive expressions to them.
In a server build, the same JSX source compiles to server-rendering operations.
There is no virtual DOM value that Solid repeatedly rebuilds and compares.

A component function runs when Solid mounts that component.
Compiled JSX invokes component functions through `createComponent`, which runs the function untracked so reactive reads in the component body do not subscribe the parent computation.
Reactive expressions created by the component track their own dependencies.

```tsx
import { createSignal } from "solid-js";

function Counter() {
	const [count, setCount] = createSignal(0);

	return (
		<button onClick={() => setCount(count() + 1)}>Count: {count()}</button>
	);
}
```

The component sets up its signal and returns its JSX when mounted.
The expression `{count()}` runs in a tracking scope and updates when `count` changes.
The event handler runs when the button is clicked.

## Props

Parents pass data and configuration to child components through JSX props.
The child receives those props as one object.
Compiled component props can use getters so an expression such as `name={selected().name}` stays reactive until the receiving component reads `props.name`.
Read reactive props inside JSX, a memo, or an effect.

```tsx
type GreetingProps = {
	name: string;
	punctuation?: string;
};

function Greeting(props: GreetingProps) {
	return (
		<p>
			Hello, {props.name}
			{props.punctuation ?? "."}
		</p>
	);
}
```

Reading a reactive prop into a local variable at the top level of a component captures that read outside a tracking scope.
Solid 2.0 warns about this pattern in development, including destructuring reactive props in the parameter list.
Keep the props object intact, or derive a local value with `createMemo` when it needs its own reactive identity.

```tsx
import { createMemo } from "solid-js";

function FullName(props: { first: string; last: string }) {
	const fullName = createMemo(() => `${props.first} ${props.last}`);
	return <span>{fullName()}</span>;
}
```

See the [`Component`, `ParentProps`, and `FlowProps` types](https://v2-rebuild--solid-docs-v2.netlify.app/reference/solid-js/types/component-types) for typing component contracts.

## Handling events

Pass a function to a camelCase event prop such as `onClick` or `onInput`.
Solid delegates supported events through the owning render or hydration root.
The handler runs when the browser dispatches the event, so reactive reads inside the handler use current values.

```tsx
function SaveButton(props: { onSave: () => void }) {
	return (
		<button type="button" onClick={() => props.onSave()}>
			Save
		</button>
	);
}
```

Use a `ref` directive with `addEventListener` when you need native listener options such as capture or passive handling.

## Refs and directives

A `ref` callback receives an element after Solid creates it.
Use the callback to keep an element reference or apply behavior that needs the DOM node.

```tsx
function SearchField() {
	let input!: HTMLInputElement;

	return (
		<>
			<input ref={(element) => (input = element)} type="search" />
			<button type="button" onClick={() => input.select()}>
				Select query
			</button>
		</>
	);
}
```

Solid 2 uses ref callbacks and directive factories instead of the `use:` namespace.
A directive factory creates owned reactive primitives during component setup and returns the callback that applies the directive to an element.

Ref callbacks run untracked and without an owner.
Do not create effects or register cleanup inside the returned callback.
Create them in the factory, where they belong to the component owner.

```tsx
import { onSettled } from "solid-js";

function listen(
	type: string,
	listener: EventListener,
	options?: AddEventListenerOptions
) {
	let element: HTMLElement | undefined;

	onSettled(() => {
		const target = element;
		if (!target) return;

		target.addEventListener(type, listener, options);
		return () => target.removeEventListener(type, listener, options);
	});

	return (next: HTMLElement) => {
		element = next;
	};
}
```

The factory registers setup and cleanup while it has an owner.
The returned callback only stores the element for the settled work.

The `ref` prop also accepts an array.
Solid recursively flattens the array and calls each callback in order, so separate directives can share one element.

```tsx
function autofocus(element: HTMLInputElement) {
	element.autofocus = true;
}

function SearchField(props: { onInput: EventListener }) {
	let input!: HTMLInputElement;

	return (
		<>
			<input
				type="search"
				ref={[
					(element) => (input = element),
					autofocus,
					listen("input", props.onInput, { passive: true }),
				]}
			/>
			<button type="button" onClick={() => input.select()}>
				Select query
			</button>
		</>
	);
}
```

Use a ref array to compose element access, reusable directives, and third-party integrations without creating one wrapper callback.
Ref callback return values are ignored; register cleanup through an owned primitive such as `onSettled`.
See the [`ref` reference](https://v2-rebuild--solid-docs-v2.netlify.app/reference/solid-web/jsx-properties/ref) for its accepted values and callback behavior.

## Classes

Use the `class` prop for static and conditional class names.
Solid 2 accepts strings, objects, and nested arrays:

```tsx
function SaveButton(props: {
	class?: string;
	active: boolean;
	saving: boolean;
}) {
	return (
		<button
			class={[
				"button",
				props.class,
				{
					active: props.active,
					"saving muted": props.saving,
				},
			]}
		>
			Save
		</button>
	);
}
```

* A string supplies the complete class value or one always-present array entry.
* An object adds each key whose value is truthy.
  A key can contain several space-separated class names.
* An array combines strings, objects, and other arrays.

Put conditional names in an object instead of constructing a string with concatenation, `filter(Boolean)`, or `join`.
Solid can then add and remove the affected class tokens directly.
The `classList` prop from Solid 1 is replaced by these object and array forms of `class`.
See the [`class` reference](https://v2-rebuild--solid-docs-v2.netlify.app/reference/solid-web/jsx-properties/class) for each supported value form.

## Children and composition

A component accepts children only when its props type includes a `children` property.
Use `ParentProps` for optional element children or write a specific children type for a render callback.

Most wrapper components can render `props.children` directly.

```tsx
import type { ParentProps } from "solid-js";

function Panel(props: ParentProps<{ title: string }>) {
	return (
		<section>
			<h2>{props.title}</h2>
			<div>{props.children}</div>
		</section>
	);
}
```

Use the [`children` helper](https://v2-rebuild--solid-docs-v2.netlify.app/reference/solid-js/components-context/children) when a component must resolve, inspect, or iterate over its children.
It returns an accessor and adds `toArray()` for iteration.

```tsx
import { children, type ParentProps } from "solid-js";

function Stack(props: ParentProps) {
	const resolved = children(() => props.children);
	return <div class="stack">{resolved.toArray()}</div>;
}
```

Composition can also use a function child when the parent needs to provide a value to the nested JSX.
The control-flow components use this pattern for narrowed values and list rows.

## Context

Context passes a value through a component subtree without forwarding it through every intermediate component.
Use it when a value belongs to one subtree and several descendants need that value.
For state that is truly application-wide and does not need subtree scoping, a module-level signal or store can be simpler.
[`createContext`](https://v2-rebuild--solid-docs-v2.netlify.app/reference/solid-js/components-context/create-context) returns a context that is also its provider component.
[`useContext`](https://v2-rebuild--solid-docs-v2.netlify.app/reference/solid-js/components-context/use-context) reads the value associated with the current owner.

```tsx
import { createContext, useContext, type ParentProps } from "solid-js";

type Theme = "light" | "dark";

const ThemeContext = createContext<Theme>("light");

function ThemeProvider(props: ParentProps<{ value: Theme }>) {
	return <ThemeContext value={props.value}>{props.children}</ThemeContext>;
}

function ThemeButton() {
	const theme = useContext(ThemeContext);
	return <button class={theme}>Save</button>;
}
```

When a context has a default value, `useContext` returns that value outside a matching provider.
When it has no default, reading it outside a matching provider throws `ContextNotFoundError`.
Providers create a scoped owner, so nested providers can replace a value for their own descendants.

## Rendering lists

Use [`For`](https://v2-rebuild--solid-docs-v2.netlify.app/reference/solid-js/components-jsx/for) when rows come from an array.
Its default keyed mode reuses the mapped row for an item with the same identity.
The callback receives the raw item and a reactive index accessor.

```tsx
import { For, createSignal } from "solid-js";

type Todo = { id: number; text: string };

function TodoList() {
	const [todos] = createSignal<Todo[]>([
		{ id: 1, text: "Read the guide" },
		{ id: 2, text: "Build an example" },
	]);

	return (
		<ul>
			<For each={todos()} fallback={<li>No tasks</li>}>
				{(todo, index) => (
					<li>
						{index() + 1}. {todo.text}
					</li>
				)}
			</For>
		</ul>
	);
}
```

Set `keyed={false}` for position-based mapping.
That form receives an item accessor and a stable numeric index.
Pass a key function when identity should come from part of each item; that form provides accessors for both the item and index.

Use [`Repeat`](https://v2-rebuild--solid-docs-v2.netlify.app/reference/solid-js/components-jsx/repeat) for positional rendering over a store.
`Repeat` creates rows from a numeric range instead of diffing an array or item identities.
Each row reads its store position directly, so a store update can notify only the expressions that read the changed properties.

Set `from` and `count` to render a sliding window without creating a sliced array.
When the window moves, `Repeat` preserves rows whose indexes remain in range, disposes rows that leave it, and creates rows for the new indexes.

```tsx
import { Repeat, createSignal, createStore } from "solid-js";

function ActivityLog() {
	const [rows] = createStore(
		Array.from({ length: 1_000 }, (_, id) => ({
			id,
			message: `Activity ${id}`,
		}))
	);
	const [from, setFrom] = createSignal(0);
	const size = 20;

	return (
		<>
			<button
				onClick={() =>
					setFrom((index) => Math.min(index + 1, rows.length - size))
				}
			>
				Next
			</button>
			<ul>
				<Repeat from={from()} count={Math.min(size, rows.length - from())}>
					{(index) => <li>{rows[index].message}</li>}
				</Repeat>
			</ul>
		</>
	);
}
```

## Conditional content

[`Show`](https://v2-rebuild--solid-docs-v2.netlify.app/reference/solid-js/components-jsx/show) renders its children when `when` is truthy and renders `fallback` otherwise.
Its default function-child form receives an accessor for the narrowed value and preserves the child while `when` remains truthy.

```tsx
import { Show, createSignal } from "solid-js";

type User = { name: string };

function Account() {
	const [user] = createSignal<User | undefined>({ name: "Ada" });

	return (
		<Show when={user()} fallback={<a href="/sign-in">Sign in</a>}>
			{(current) => <p>Signed in as {current().name}</p>}
		</Show>
	);
}
```

With `keyed`, the callback receives the raw narrowed value and the child remounts when that value changes identity.

Use [`Switch` and `Match`](https://v2-rebuild--solid-docs-v2.netlify.app/reference/solid-js/components-jsx/switch-and-match) when several conditions are mutually exclusive.
`Switch` renders the first truthy `Match`, or its fallback when none match.
Function children follow the same keyed and non-keyed value rules as `Show`.

```tsx
import { Match, Switch } from "solid-js";

function Status(props: { code: number }) {
	return (
		<Switch fallback={<p>Unknown status</p>}>
			<Match when={props.code === 200}>
				<p>Ready</p>
			</Match>
			<Match when={props.code === 404}>
				<p>Not found</p>
			</Match>
		</Switch>
	);
}
```

## Dynamic components

Import `dynamic` from `@solidjs/web`.
`dynamic()` is the canonical API for selecting a component or native element from a reactive source.
It returns a stable component reference that forwards props and children.

```tsx
import { createSignal, type Component } from "solid-js";
import { dynamic } from "@solidjs/web";

const Compact: Component<{ value: string }> = (props) => (
	<span>{props.value}</span>
);
const Detailed: Component<{ value: string }> = (props) => (
	<strong>{props.value}</strong>
);

const [detailed, setDetailed] = createSignal(false);
const Result = dynamic(() => (detailed() ? Detailed : Compact));

export function Preview() {
	return (
		<>
			<button onClick={() => setDetailed((value) => !value)}>
				Toggle detail
			</button>
			<Result value="Current result" />
		</>
	);
}
```

The source may also resolve to a native tag name or an async component.
See the [`dynamic()` reference](https://v2-rebuild--solid-docs-v2.netlify.app/reference/solid-web/components/dynamic).
---

# Async reactivity

Source: https://v2-rebuild--solid-docs-v2.netlify.app/concepts/async-reactivity.md

Solid computations can return promises and async iterables.
Consumers still read their accessors as reactive values instead of receiving the promise or iterator.

## Settled view and in-flight work

Separate the answer currently visible to the user from the work that may produce another answer:

![A timeline showing a Loading fallback during the first request, Answer A remaining visible during a held update, and Answer B appearing when the next request settles.](https://v2-rebuild--solid-docs-v2.netlify.app/images/diagrams/async-update-timeline.svg)

Before the first answer settles, the UI has no value to render.
After an answer settles, Solid holds the current committed view while work prepares the next answer.
Other writes in the same update wait with it, then commit together when the pending work settles.
This held update is automatic and does not require a `Loading` boundary.
The boundary controls what renders when no settled answer exists; it does not create the hold.
Each async primitive closes a specific gap in this timeline instead of creating a separate async state model.

## No settled answer: `Loading`

Reading an async source before its first result reports that the value is not ready.
A [`Loading` boundary](https://v2-rebuild--solid-docs-v2.netlify.app/reference/solid-js/components-jsx/loading) turns that state into fallback UI.

```tsx
import { Loading, createMemo, createSignal } from "solid-js";

type User = {
	id: number;
	name: string;
	bio: string;
};

function UserProfile() {
	const [id, setId] = createSignal(1);
	const user = createMemo(async (): Promise<User> => {
		const requestedId = id();
		const response = await fetch(`/api/users/${requestedId}`);

		if (!response.ok) {
			throw new Error(`Could not load user ${requestedId}`);
		}

		return response.json();
	});

	return (
		<section>
			<button onClick={() => setId((current) => current + 1)}>Next user</button>
			<Loading fallback={<p>Loading profile...</p>}>
				<article>
					<h2>{user().name}</h2>
					<p>{user().bio}</p>
				</article>
			</Loading>
		</section>
	);
}
```

The first read of `user()` has no value, so `Loading` shows its fallback.
When the request resolves, the computation commits the answer and the content renders.

## Another answer is coming: `isPending`

After the first answer settles, changing `id()` asks a new question while the current profile remains visible.
Use [`isPending(fn)`](https://v2-rebuild--solid-docs-v2.netlify.app/reference/solid-js/reactivity/is-pending) when that visible content needs an updating indicator:

```tsx
import { Loading, isPending } from "solid-js";

<Loading fallback={<p>Loading profile...</p>}>
	<article
		aria-busy={isPending(user) ? "true" : "false"}
		class={{ updating: isPending(user) }}
	>
		<h2>{user().name}</h2>
		<p>{user().bio}</p>
	</article>
</Loading>;
```

`isPending` evaluates the expression passed to it.
The result becomes `true` when another answer for that expression is in flight and has not revealed.
If the expression has no settled answer yet, its read still follows the surrounding `Loading` path.

## Work rejects: `Errored`

If async work rejects, the error travels through the reactive graph.
An [`Errored` boundary](https://v2-rebuild--solid-docs-v2.netlify.app/reference/solid-js/components-jsx/errored) turns an unhandled error into recoverable UI:

```tsx
import { Errored, Loading } from "solid-js";

<Errored
	fallback={(error, reset) => (
		<section>
			<p>{String(error())}</p>
			<button onClick={reset}>Retry</button>
		</section>
	)}
>
	<Loading fallback={<p>Loading profile...</p>}>
		<UserDetails user={user()} />
	</Loading>
</Errored>;
```

`Loading` and `Errored` handle separate states.
A loading boundary does not consume errors, and an error boundary does not replace loading UI.

## Coordinate a mutation: `action` and optimistic state

Reads usually need no imperative wrapper because changing their tracked inputs starts the next async computation.
Mutations have a different shape because writes can happen before and after a server round trip.

[`action(generator)`](https://v2-rebuild--solid-docs-v2.netlify.app/reference/solid-js/lifecycle-actions/action) runs a generator or async generator as a transaction.
Each `yield` waits for its value and restores the action's transaction before the generator continues.
The returned action is an async function intended for event handlers and other imperative scopes.

[`createOptimistic`](https://v2-rebuild--solid-docs-v2.netlify.app/reference/solid-js/reactivity/create-optimistic) and [`createOptimisticStore`](https://v2-rebuild--solid-docs-v2.netlify.app/reference/solid-js/stores/create-optimistic-store) provide writable state for the expected result of a mutation.
Writes are visible immediately inside an action and are tentative until its transaction finishes.
The authoritative async result can reconcile the optimistic value, and a failed action reverts the tentative write.

The common mutation timeline is:

1. Start an `action`.
2. Write the expected result to optimistic state.
3. `yield` the durable operation.
4. Call [`refresh(source)`](https://v2-rebuild--solid-docs-v2.netlify.app/reference/solid-js/lifecycle-actions/refresh) to read durable truth again.
5. Settle the action and remove its optimistic overlay.

`action` coordinates this process.
Optimistic state supplies the predicted change while the operation is in flight.
`refresh` reconciles the visible result with the authoritative source.

### Layer state by lifetime

An async feature can combine state with different lifetimes.
One useful composition applies three layers in a fixed order:

1. **Persistent data** comes from the async projection.
   A server, browser database, or another durable source owns this layer.
2. **Ephemeral UI state** records recoverable errors, drafts, or notices that should survive an optimistic rollback.
   The projection applies this layer to the persistent result.
3. **Optimistic state** represents the expected result of the active mutation.
   `createOptimisticStore` applies this action-scoped layer last and removes it when the action settles.

Consumers read the composed store rather than choosing between separate persistent and optimistic views.
The primitive composition enforces the layer order.

```tsx
import {
	Errored,
	For,
	Loading,
	Show,
	action,
	createOptimisticStore,
	refresh,
} from "solid-js";
import { api } from "./api";

type ServerTodo = {
	id: string;
	title: string;
	completed: boolean;
};

type TodoError = {
	completed: boolean;
};

type Todo = ServerTodo & {
	pending?: boolean;
	error?: TodoError;
};

function createTodos() {
	const errors = new Map<string, TodoError>();
	const [todos, setTodos] = createOptimisticStore<Todo[]>(async () => {
		const current: ServerTodo[] = await api.getTodos();
		return current.map((todo) => {
			const error = errors.get(todo.id);
			return error ? { ...todo, error } : todo;
		});
	}, []);

	const toggleTodo = action(function* (id: string, completed: boolean) {
		setTodos((draft) => {
			const todo = draft.find((todo) => todo.id === id);
			if (!todo) return;
			todo.completed = completed;
			todo.pending = true;
		});

		try {
			yield api.toggleTodo(id, completed);
			errors.delete(id);
		} catch {
			errors.set(id, { completed });
		} finally {
			refresh(todos);
		}
	});

	return [todos, { toggleTodo }] as const;
}

function TodoList() {
	const [todos, { toggleTodo }] = createTodos();

	return (
		<Errored fallback={(error) => <p>{String(error())}</p>}>
			<Loading fallback={<p>Loading todos...</p>}>
				<ul>
					<For each={todos}>
						{(todo) => (
							<li
								class={{
									pending: !!todo.pending,
									errored: !!todo.error,
								}}
							>
								<label>
									<input
										type="checkbox"
										checked={todo.completed}
										onInput={(event) =>
											void toggleTodo(todo.id, event.currentTarget.checked)
										}
									/>
									{todo.title}
								</label>
								<Show when={todo.error}>
									{(error) => (
										<button
											type="button"
											onClick={() =>
												void toggleTodo(todo.id, error().completed)
											}
										>
											Retry
										</button>
									)}
								</Show>
							</li>
						)}
					</For>
				</ul>
			</Loading>
		</Errored>
	);
}
```

`createTodos()` creates all three layers under the component owner and returns one composed store with its actions.
This avoids module-scope reactive state and cross-request sharing.

The projection reads the durable todos and folds in the `errors` map.
The map is not reactive by itself; `refresh(todos)` reruns the projection after each mutation and makes its current entries visible.

The optimistic write updates `completed` and adds `pending` before the request finishes.
On success, the refreshed persistent result confirms the update.
On failure, the optimistic layer reverts and reveals the persistent todo with its retry state from the projection.

The action catches an expected mutation failure because the row can recover locally.
An unhandled projection or render error still reaches `Errored`.

## Additional control

### Mark data as changing: `affects`

Optimistic writes and pending declarations answer separate questions.
An optimistic field such as `pending` describes the mutation process, while the optimistic data supplies the expected value to display.
[`affects(target, key?)`](https://v2-rebuild--solid-docs-v2.netlify.app/reference/solid-js/lifecycle-actions/affects) describes the state of the targeted data.
Consumers read that source, store, or store property as pending while work that can change it remains in flight.

These mechanisms are independent and can be used together.
Use both when the UI needs an expected value and data-level pending status for the same mutation.
Optimism does not remove the value of `affects`, and `affects` does not require the affected data to lack an optimistic value.

A bare `refresh(source)` quietly asks the same question again because the visible answer still matches the source's inputs.
Pair `affects(source)` with `refresh(source)` when the reload itself should make consumers report pending.

### Read a speculative value: `latest`

Normal reads preserve the visible settled answer while a held update prepares its replacement.
Use [`latest(fn)`](https://v2-rebuild--solid-docs-v2.netlify.app/reference/solid-js/reactivity/latest) when a preview or coordination path needs the freshest in-flight value:

```tsx
import { latest } from "solid-js";

const nextUser = () => latest(user);
```

If the next answer exists, `latest(user)` returns it before the held update commits it.
Otherwise, it returns the visible settled answer.
Before any answer exists, the read still follows the normal `Loading` path.

A common use case for `latest` is an autocomplete or typeahead interface that keeps results visible while the next value loads.

### Declare a committed first value

Most applications should let unresolved first reads reach a `Loading` boundary.
Use `loadingValue` only when a signal-family source must return a declared committed value during its first async flight.
Use `seedLoadingValue: true` for the equivalent projected-store case.

These escape hatches bypass `Loading` for the first flight.
They remain `isPending`-false until the first computed value lands, then later updates follow the normal pending behavior.
See the [`createMemo`](https://v2-rebuild--solid-docs-v2.netlify.app/reference/solid-js/reactivity/create-memo) and [`createStore`](https://v2-rebuild--solid-docs-v2.netlify.app/reference/solid-js/stores/create-store) references for their complete option contracts.

## Queue completion

Reactive writes normally schedule one batched update on the microtask queue.
[`flush()`](https://v2-rebuild--solid-docs-v2.netlify.app/reference/solid-js/reactivity/flush) drains that queue synchronously, while `flush(fn)` runs the callback in a synchronous flush scope and drains before returning.
This is useful in tests and imperative integrations that must observe a queued write before the next microtask.

`flush` does not replace async coordination.
Use [`resolve(fn)`](https://v2-rebuild--solid-docs-v2.netlify.app/reference/solid-js/advanced/interop-async/resolve) outside a tracking scope to await one reactive expression.
It resolves with the first settled value or rejects with the expression's error.

Use [`onSettled(callback)`](https://v2-rebuild--solid-docs-v2.netlify.app/reference/solid-js/lifecycle-actions/on-settled) to register one callback after pending async reads under the current owner resolve and the reactive queue flushes.
Reads inside the callback are untracked, so another settle requires another registration.
When registered in an owned scope, the callback can return a cleanup function for owner disposal.

```tsx
import { onSettled } from "solid-js";

function SearchResults() {
	let searchInput!: HTMLInputElement;

	onSettled(() => {
		const controller = new AbortController();
		const focusSearch = (event: KeyboardEvent) => {
			if (event.key === "/") {
				event.preventDefault();
				searchInput.focus();
			}
		};

		window.addEventListener("keydown", focusSearch, {
			signal: controller.signal,
		});

		return () => controller.abort();
	});

	return (
		<input
			ref={(element) => (searchInput = element)}
			type="search"
			aria-label="Search"
		/>
	);
}
```

## Tracking across async gaps

Dependency tracking is synchronous.
An async computation registers reactive reads made before its first `await`.
Reads made after an `await` do not create dependency edges, so later updates to those sources cannot invalidate the computation.

Read every reactive input before the first async gap.

```ts
const profile = createMemo(async () => {
	const userId = id();
	const permission = permissions();

	const response = await fetch(`/api/users/${userId}`);
	const user = await response.json();

	return { user, permission };
});
```

:::caution[First unresolved read after await]
A source first read after `await` cannot notify the async computation when it settles because no dependency edge exists.
At this revision, development builds convert that unresolved read into an error that can reach `Errored`.
Production builds do not include this authorship check, so this unsupported pattern can remain pending without a retry.
A post-`await` re-read can retry only when the same source was already tracked before the first `await`.
:::

Async generators used with `action` have a related transaction boundary.
A plain `await` does not restore the action transaction for writes to sources that the action has not written earlier.
Use `yield promise` as the suspension point, or place a bare `yield` before writing after an `await`.

## Capability map

* Return a promise or async iterable from a computation to create an async reactive source.
* Use [`Loading`](https://v2-rebuild--solid-docs-v2.netlify.app/reference/solid-js/components-jsx/loading) when no settled answer exists.
* Use [`isPending`](https://v2-rebuild--solid-docs-v2.netlify.app/reference/solid-js/reactivity/is-pending) when visible data has an unrevealed replacement or an `affects` declaration.
* Use [`Errored`](https://v2-rebuild--solid-docs-v2.netlify.app/reference/solid-js/components-jsx/errored) to handle errors that reach the reactive graph.
* Use [`action`](https://v2-rebuild--solid-docs-v2.netlify.app/reference/solid-js/lifecycle-actions/action) to coordinate writes across async gaps.
* Use [`createOptimistic`](https://v2-rebuild--solid-docs-v2.netlify.app/reference/solid-js/reactivity/create-optimistic) or [`createOptimisticStore`](https://v2-rebuild--solid-docs-v2.netlify.app/reference/solid-js/stores/create-optimistic-store) to show an expected result until an action settles.
* Use [`refresh`](https://v2-rebuild--solid-docs-v2.netlify.app/reference/solid-js/lifecycle-actions/refresh) to rerun a derived source with the same inputs.
* Use [`affects`](https://v2-rebuild--solid-docs-v2.netlify.app/reference/solid-js/lifecycle-actions/affects) to declare which data in-flight work can change.
* Use [`latest`](https://v2-rebuild--solid-docs-v2.netlify.app/reference/solid-js/reactivity/latest) to read the freshest available in-flight value.
* Use [`resolve`](https://v2-rebuild--solid-docs-v2.netlify.app/reference/solid-js/advanced/interop-async/resolve) to await one reactive expression outside tracking.
* Use [`onSettled`](https://v2-rebuild--solid-docs-v2.netlify.app/reference/solid-js/lifecycle-actions/on-settled) to run code after owned async work and queued updates settle.
* Use `loadingValue` or `seedLoadingValue` only when a source needs a committed value before its first result.
---

# Boundaries

Source: https://v2-rebuild--solid-docs-v2.netlify.app/concepts/boundaries.md

Boundaries turn status from the reactive graph into renderable UI.
`Loading` handles unresolved async reads, `Errored` handles errors, and `Reveal` coordinates when sibling loading regions become visible.

A boundary handles status produced by the reactive work in its subtree.
The nearest matching boundary handles that status.
Loading and error status remain separate, so a `Loading` boundary does not hide errors and an `Errored` boundary does not replace loading UI.
This lets both boundaries protect the same region.

```tsx
import { Errored, Loading, createMemo, createSignal } from "solid-js";

type Account = {
	id: number;
	name: string;
};

function AccountPanel() {
	const [accountId, setAccountId] = createSignal(1);
	const account = createMemo(async (): Promise<Account> => {
		const id = accountId();
		const response = await fetch(`/api/accounts/${id}`);

		if (!response.ok) {
			throw new Error(`Could not load account ${id}`);
		}

		return response.json();
	});

	return (
		<>
			<button onClick={() => setAccountId((id) => id + 1)}>Next account</button>
			<Errored
				fallback={(error, reset) => (
					<section>
						<p>{String(error())}</p>
						<button onClick={reset}>Retry</button>
					</section>
				)}
			>
				<Loading on={accountId()} fallback={<p>Loading account...</p>}>
					<h2>{account().name}</h2>
				</Loading>
			</Errored>
		</>
	);
}
```

The first unresolved read is handled by `Loading`.
A rejected request passes through `Loading` and is handled by `Errored`.
Changing `accountId()` while the new request is pending changes the `on` value, so the loading fallback can appear for the new account.
The navigation button remains outside both fallback regions, so it stays available while the account panel changes.

## Loading boundaries

[`Loading`](https://v2-rebuild--solid-docs-v2.netlify.app/reference/solid-js/components-jsx/loading) renders its `fallback` while async values read by its subtree have not produced the content the boundary needs.
It observes not-ready reads from computations reached through that subtree.
The nearest loading boundary that is eligible for the update owns the fallback.

After a boundary has displayed content, it normally keeps that content visible during later async updates.
Use `isPending` in the content for an updating indicator.
Use the optional `on` prop when a change to a particular value should make an initialized boundary eligible to show its fallback again.

The `on` prop is a value, not an accessor passed by reference.
For example, `on={accountId()}` compares account identifiers across pending updates.
Pending work caused by other values leaves the initialized content in place.

Place a loading boundary around the smallest coherent region that its fallback should replace.
Keep navigation, forms, and other controls outside when they must remain available during the load.
Use nested loading boundaries when independent regions should become visible as soon as their own data is ready.

## Error boundaries

[`Errored`](https://v2-rebuild--solid-docs-v2.netlify.app/reference/solid-js/components-jsx/errored) catches uncaught errors reported by its subtree and renders fallback content.
Async rejections and synchronous computation errors use the same error status path.

Place an error boundary around the smallest region that can fail and recover as one unit.
Content outside that region remains visible when the fallback appears.

The fallback can be a static element or a function.
The function receives an accessor for the captured error and a `reset` function.
Calling `reset` retries the reactive sources collected by the boundary so the branch can render again.

An error thrown while rendering an `Errored` fallback is outside that boundary's protected subtree.
A parent `Errored` boundary can catch it.
Without a matching error boundary, an unhandled error is escalated instead of being consumed by a loading boundary.

## Reveal order

[`Reveal`](https://v2-rebuild--solid-docs-v2.netlify.app/reference/solid-js/components-jsx/reveal) coordinates loading boundaries created directly within it.
It changes reveal timing without changing which async sources each `Loading` boundary observes.
It does not fetch data or create loading state.
Use it when independent regions should appear in a deliberate order instead of revealing in network completion order.

The default `sequential` order reveals slots in registration order.
A later slot stays on its fallback until every earlier slot is ready.
With `collapsed`, sequential slots after the current frontier render no fallback, so only the frontier fallback remains visible.

The `together` order holds every direct slot until all direct slots are minimally ready, then releases the group together.
For a loading slot, minimal readiness means its content is ready.
For a nested reveal group, minimal readiness follows that group's own order.

The `natural` order lets each loading slot reveal when its own data resolves.
At the top level this matches leaving the loading boundaries uncoordinated.
Its purpose is nesting, where the natural group participates as one composite slot in an outer order.

```tsx
import { Loading, Reveal } from "solid-js";

function ProfilePage() {
	return (
		<Reveal>
			<Loading fallback={<HeaderSkeleton />}>
				<ProfileHeader />
			</Loading>
			<Reveal order="natural">
				<Loading fallback={<CardSkeleton />}>
					<RecentPosts />
				</Loading>
				<Loading fallback={<CardSkeleton />}>
					<Recommendations />
				</Loading>
			</Reveal>
			<Loading fallback={<FooterSkeleton />}>
				<ProfileFooter />
			</Loading>
		</Reveal>
	);
}
```

The outer group uses sequential order.
The nested group occupies one position between the header and footer.
When the outer frontier releases that position, the two inner loading regions follow natural order and reveal independently.
The footer waits until the nested group is fully ready.

### Which boundaries join a reveal group

A `Loading` boundary joins the nearest `Reveal` present when that boundary is created.
A nested loading or error boundary starts a separate boundary scope for its subtree.
As a result, loading boundaries nested inside another `Loading` do not become additional slots in the outer reveal group.
A loading boundary wrapped by `Errored` also does not delay an ancestor reveal group.

A nested `Reveal` is different because it registers its controller as one composite slot with the parent reveal group.
An outer hold propagates through that nested group, keeping its descendant loading boundaries on their fallbacks until the parent releases the composite slot.
The inner group then resumes its own order.

These ownership rules make reveal membership structural.
Wrapping a descendant in another loading boundary does not let it escape an outer reveal hold.
Move the region outside the outer `Reveal` when it must reveal independently.

## Primitive forms

The JSX components are built from lower-level primitives.
Use the primitives when implementing custom boundary components or renderer-level integrations.

[`createLoadingBoundary(fn, fallback, options?)`](https://v2-rebuild--solid-docs-v2.netlify.app/reference/solid-js/advanced/jsx-component-primitives/create-loading-boundary) returns an accessor whose value switches between the tracked `fn` and the fallback.
Its `on` option accepts an accessor because the primitive does not receive JSX props.

[`createErrorBoundary(fn, fallback)`](https://v2-rebuild--solid-docs-v2.netlify.app/reference/solid-js/advanced/jsx-component-primitives/create-error-boundary) returns an accessor and passes the fallback an error accessor plus a reset function.

[`createRevealOrder(fn, options?)`](https://v2-rebuild--solid-docs-v2.netlify.app/reference/solid-js/advanced/jsx-component-primitives/create-reveal-order) runs `fn` under a reveal controller.
Its `order` and `collapsed` options are accessors, and nested controllers use the same composite-slot rules as `Reveal`.

```tsx
import { createErrorBoundary, createLoadingBoundary } from "solid-js";

function StatusBoundary(props: {
	children: JSX.Element;
	loading: JSX.Element;
}) {
	const output = createErrorBoundary(
		() =>
			createLoadingBoundary(
				() => props.children,
				() => props.loading
			)(),
		(error, reset) => (
			<section>
				<p>{String(error())}</p>
				<button onClick={reset}>Retry</button>
			</section>
		)
	);

	return output() as JSX.Element;
}
```
---

# Rendering and SSR

Source: https://v2-rebuild--solid-docs-v2.netlify.app/concepts/rendering-and-ssr.md

Solid uses the same component source for client rendering and server-side rendering (SSR).
The JSX build target selects DOM operations for the browser or HTML-producing operations for the server.

## Client rendering

Use [`render`](https://v2-rebuild--solid-docs-v2.netlify.app/reference/solid-web/rendering-ssr/render) to mount a client-rendered tree into a DOM container.
It returns a disposer that tears down the tree and its reactive scopes.

```tsx
import { render } from "@solidjs/web";
import { App } from "./App";

const root = document.getElementById("app");

if (!root) {
	throw new Error("Missing #app");
}

const dispose = render(() => <App />, root);

// Call dispose() when this root must be unmounted.
```

Pass a function to `render` so Solid creates the root before it evaluates the component tree.
The root owns delegated event listeners for its container, and disposal removes those listeners.
The initial attachment is scheduled through Solid's effect queue.
When the initial render has no unresolved async read, `render` flushes that work before returning.
If an uncaught async read is pending, the initial mount waits and attaches after it settles.

## Hydrating server HTML

Use [`hydrate`](https://v2-rebuild--solid-docs-v2.netlify.app/reference/solid-web/rendering-ssr/hydrate) when the container already holds HTML produced by `renderToString` or `renderToStream`.
Hydration claims the existing server-rendered nodes and attaches event handlers and reactive bindings without reconstructing those nodes.
It also returns a disposer for the reactive tree, while leaving the DOM nodes in place.

```tsx
import { hydrate } from "@solidjs/web";
import { App } from "./App";

const root = document.getElementById("app");

if (!root) {
	throw new Error("Missing #app");
}

hydrate(() => <App />, root);
```

The server and client must render the same initial structure in each hydrated region.
Solid assigns hydration keys and serializes hydration state during SSR so the client build can claim the matching nodes.
When the application owns the full document, include `HydrationScript` once before the application markup.
It initializes hydration support and captures configured delegated events that occur before the client bundle hydrates.
For multiple roots, give each server render a distinct `renderId` and pass the same value to its `hydrate` call.

```tsx
// Server
const accountHtml = renderToString(() => <Account />, { renderId: "account" });

// Client
hydrate(() => <Account />, accountRoot, { renderId: "account" });
```

## Synchronous string rendering

[`renderToString`](https://v2-rebuild--solid-docs-v2.netlify.app/reference/solid-web/rendering-ssr/render-to-string) runs a component tree synchronously and returns an HTML string.
Use it when the tree can complete synchronously or when pending content is enclosed by a `Loading` boundary whose fallback is suitable for the response.

```tsx
import { Loading } from "solid-js";
import { renderToString } from "@solidjs/web";
import { App } from "./App";

const html = renderToString(() => (
	<Loading fallback={<main>Loading…</main>}>
		<App />
	</Loading>
));
```

When a read is pending inside `Loading`, string rendering emits that boundary's fallback.
Use streaming when the client must receive content after asynchronous work settles.

## Streaming rendering

[`renderToStream`](https://v2-rebuild--solid-docs-v2.netlify.app/reference/solid-web/rendering-ssr/render-to-stream) emits the synchronous shell first.
It then emits fragments as pending `Loading` boundaries settle.

The returned object can pipe to a Node writable, pipe to a Web `WritableStream`, expose a `ReadableStream<Uint8Array>`, or be awaited for the fully settled HTML.
Choose one output form for each render.

```tsx
import { renderToStream } from "@solidjs/web";
import { App } from "./App";

export function handleRequest(): Response {
	const stream = renderToStream(() => <App />);
	return new Response(stream.readable, {
		headers: { "content-type": "text/html; charset=utf-8" },
	});
}
```

Awaiting the result collects the completed render as a string.

```tsx
const html = await renderToStream(() => <App />);
```

Async reads without a surrounding `Loading` boundary block the shell until they settle.
Inside a boundary, the shell can contain the fallback and a later stream fragment can replace it.
The [Boundaries concept page](https://v2-rebuild--solid-docs-v2.netlify.app/concepts/boundaries) explains `Loading`, `Errored`, and reveal ordering.

The [async reactivity page](https://v2-rebuild--solid-docs-v2.netlify.app/concepts/async-reactivity) covers the advanced escape hatch for rendering a declared first value instead of a boundary fallback.

## Who owns the document

A server render can produce a complete document or a fragment embedded in a document owned by another host.
This choice determines how head content and render assets reach the document.

When the rendered output includes a closing `</head>`, the renderer inserts registered head content and assets into that document.

```tsx
import { HydrationScript, renderToString } from "@solidjs/web";
import { App } from "./App";

const html = renderToString(() => (
	<html lang="en">
		<head>
			<meta charset="utf-8" />
			<HydrationScript />
		</head>
		<body>
			<div id="app">
				<App />
			</div>
		</body>
	</html>
));
```

When another host owns the document, render the application fragment and use `onHead` to receive the head HTML.
For `renderToString`, `onHead` runs synchronously before the function returns.
For `renderToStream`, it runs before the shell is emitted.

```tsx
let head = "";

const body = renderToString(() => <App />, {
	onHead(value) {
		head = value;
	},
});

const documentHtml = `<!doctype html>
<html>
  <head>${head}</head>
  <body><div id="app">${body}</div></body>
</html>`;
```

The renderer manages HTML generation and head delivery in these examples.
Server adapters, routing, response creation, and deployment belong to the application layer.

## Controlling hydration

[`NoHydration`](https://v2-rebuild--solid-docs-v2.netlify.app/reference/solid-js/advanced/manual-hydration/no-hydration) renders its children on the server without hydration keys or serialized signals.
During client hydration, Solid skips that subtree and leaves its DOM untouched.
After hydration, the client can render the children fresh.

```tsx
import { NoHydration } from "solid-js";

<NoHydration>
	<aside>This server-rendered content is not hydrated.</aside>
</NoHydration>;
```

[`Hydration`](https://v2-rebuild--solid-docs-v2.netlify.app/reference/solid-js/advanced/manual-hydration/hydration) re-enables hydration inside a `NoHydration` region.
On the server, it establishes a new hydration ID namespace.
Pass the same value to the independent client root's `renderId`.

```tsx
import { Hydration, NoHydration } from "solid-js";

<NoHydration>
	<div id="account">
		<Hydration id="account">
			<Account />
		</Hydration>
	</div>
</NoHydration>;
```

The client can hydrate that inner region as its own root.

```tsx
import { hydrate } from "@solidjs/web";

const accountRoot = document.getElementById("account")!;

hydrate(() => <Account />, accountRoot, { renderId: "account" });
```

These controls divide hydration ownership rather than choose visible content.
Use them only when the server and client intentionally manage different parts of the document.

## Server and client boundaries

[`isServer`](https://v2-rebuild--solid-docs-v2.netlify.app/reference/solid-web/rendering-ssr/is-server) is a build-time constant.
The browser entry exports `false`, and the server entry exports `true`.
Bundlers can remove the unreachable side of an environment guard.

```tsx
import { isServer } from "@solidjs/web";

if (!isServer) {
	window.addEventListener("online", reportOnline);
}
```

Use [`clientOnly`](https://v2-rebuild--solid-docs-v2.netlify.app/reference/solid-web/rendering-ssr/client-only) for a dynamically imported component that must never execute on the server.
The server renders only its fallback and does not start the import.
The client hydrates that fallback, waits for the module and hydration to settle, and then swaps in the component.

```tsx
import { clientOnly } from "@solidjs/web";

const Map = clientOnly(() => import("./Map"));

export function Location() {
	return <Map fallback={<div>Map unavailable during SSR</div>} />;
}
```

By default, `clientOnly` starts loading when its declaration runs.
Pass `{ lazy: true }` to defer the import until the component's first render.
---

# App structure

Source: https://v2-rebuild--solid-docs-v2.netlify.app/building-apps/app-structure.md

Start mode lets `@solidjs/vite-plugin` own the application entries, development serving, and production build.
Enable it with `start: true` or the equivalent `start: {}` option:

```ts
import { defineConfig } from "vite";
import solid from "@solidjs/vite-plugin";

export default defineConfig({
	plugins: [solid({ start: true })],
});
```

A start-mode project does not need an `index.html` file or a client mount file.
When no authored entry is selected, the plugin generates those parts from `src/App.tsx` and, when present, `src/Document.tsx`.

## Rendering mode

Start mode uses client rendering unless `ssr: true` is set.
Client mode prerenders the document shell without `App` to `dist/client/index.html`, then calls `render()` to mount the app into `document.body`.
Without server functions, the build removes `dist/server` and the resulting `dist/client` directory can be deployed to a static host.
When server functions are enabled, pages remain static but their endpoint still uses the retained server handler.

Add `ssr: true` to render the app for each request:

```ts
export default defineConfig({
	plugins: [
		solid({
			start: true,
			ssr: true,
		}),
	],
});
```

SSR mode streams the initial document and calls `hydrate()` in the browser.
A production build emits browser assets under `dist/client` and a request handler under `dist/server`.
The same `App` and `Document` conventions apply to generated entries in both modes.

## The app component

`src/App.tsx` default-exports the root component used by generated entries.
The `solid-v2/bare` template keeps all application content under this component:

```tsx
import { createSignal } from "solid-js";
import logo from "./logo.svg";
import "./App.css";

export default function App() {
	const [count, setCount] = createSignal(0);

	return (
		<header class="header">
			<img src={logo} class="logo" alt="Solid logo" />
			<button onClick={() => setCount(count() + 1)}>Clicks: {count()}</button>
		</header>
	);
}
```

For `src/App`, start mode probes `.tsx`, `.jsx`, `.ts`, then `.js`.
It repeats that extension order for lowercase `src/app` only when no uppercase stem matches.
Set `start.app` to use another module inside the Vite project root.

## The document component

`src/Document.tsx` default-exports the full HTML document used by generated entries.
It receives the app as `props.children`, so the body must render those children.
An SSR-ready document includes `<HydrationScript />`:

```tsx
import type { ParentProps } from "solid-js";
import { HydrationScript } from "@solidjs/web";

export default function Document(props: ParentProps) {
	return (
		<html lang="en">
			<head>
				<meta charset="utf-8" />
				<meta name="viewport" content="width=device-width, initial-scale=1" />
				<link rel="icon" href="/favicon.ico" />
				<title>Solid App</title>
				<HydrationScript />
			</head>
			<body>{props.children}</body>
		</html>
	);
}
```

For the document convention, the plugin probes `src/Document.tsx` and then `src/Document.jsx`.
A module selected by `start.document` takes precedence over that convention.
If no document module exists, the generated entries use a minimal built-in document.
With generated entries, the plugin injects the client entry script into the document head.
In client mode, the app is omitted from the prerendered shell and any output from `<HydrationScript />` is removed because the browser mounts rather than hydrates.

## Generated and authored entries

In SSR mode, the generated server entry renders `<Document><App /></Document>` and the generated client entry hydrates the same tree.
The generation path is used when neither an explicit nor a conventional entry is selected.

The plugin probes conventional entries in `.tsx`, `.jsx`, `.ts`, `.js`, then `.mjs` order.
Explicit `start.entryServer` and `start.entryClient` paths take precedence over the corresponding conventional files.
SSR mode requires authored entries in pairs because both sides must render the same document tree.
The server entry exports `render()`, which may return a stream result, an HTML string, or a `Response`.
The authored client entry owns hydration.
An authored SSR document also owns its client script element.
The server render receives the resolved client-entry URL in its context, and the production handler also rewrites a literal root-relative reference to that authored client entry.

An authored SSR pair bypasses the `App` and `Document` conventions because those modules belong to generated entries.

Client mode always generates the server-side shell renderer.
An authored client entry can stand alone and owns the browser mount in that mode.
Authored server entries and `start.entryServer` are ignored in client mode.
The selected `Document` still supplies the client-mode shell when the client entry is authored.

## Mounting a router

Start mode does not select a router.
Mount the router or its provider under `App`, as shown by both router variants in the official templates.
The route modules and route-table generation then remain concerns of the chosen router.

Some routers need a request-specific instance to load matched routes before SSR begins.
For generated SSR entries, `start.setup` can point to a server-only module that prepares that instance:

```ts
solid({
	start: {
		setup: "./src/setup.tsx",
	},
	ssr: true,
});
```

The server-only setup module default-exports a function that receives the current request event and the `App` component.
The generated server entry invokes it after middleware dispatch reaches the page render and before `renderToStream()` starts.
The function may return a component, nothing, or a promise of either result.
A returned component renders in `App`'s place inside `Document`, while no return keeps `<App />`.
The browser-side `App` must produce the corresponding router tree so hydration matches.

`start.setup` runs only for page renders made by a generated server entry.
It is ignored in client mode.
In SSR mode, combining it with an authored server entry is an error.
---

# Styling and assets

Source: https://v2-rebuild--solid-docs-v2.netlify.app/building-apps/styling-and-assets.md

Solid source files use Vite's CSS and asset imports.
Start mode keeps those import forms for generated and authored entries.

## CSS

Import a stylesheet for its side effects to make its rules global.
The official templates import the application stylesheet from `App`:

```tsx
import "./App.css";

export default function App() {
	return <main class="content">Hello Solid</main>;
}
```

The stylesheet becomes part of the module graph at the point where it is imported.
This applies to CSS imported by a component, a route module, or `App`.
The import location does not scope ordinary CSS selectors.

## CSS modules

Name a file `*.module.css` and import its generated class-name map:

```tsx
import styles from "./route.module.css";

export default function Route() {
	return <p class={styles.message}>Scoped by CSS modules</p>;
}
```

```css
.message {
	color: rebeccapurple;
}
```

Vite replaces `styles.message` with the generated class name and includes the stylesheet in the same asset pipeline as global CSS.

## CSS preprocessors

Start mode uses Vite's preprocessor pipeline without additional start configuration.
Install the preprocessor implementation required by the file type, then import the file from application code.

The official `solid-v2/with-sass` template installs `sass`, renames `App.css` to `App.scss`, and keeps the same import:

```tsx
import "./App.scss";
```

Sass compiles to CSS during the Vite build and adds no Sass runtime to the browser.
Use the `*.module.scss` name when a Sass file also needs CSS-module class names.

## Imported asset URLs

Import an asset when application code needs its resolved URL.
The official templates import the Solid logo and pass that URL to an element:

```tsx
import logo from "../logo.svg";

export default function Home() {
	return <img src={logo} alt="Solid logo" />;
}
```

Append `?url` when a file should be imported explicitly as a URL.
This is useful when code creates the consuming element:

```tsx
import stylesheetUrl from "./theme.css?url";

export default function ThemeLink() {
	return <link rel="stylesheet" href={stylesheetUrl} />;
}
```

The official templates set `build.assetsInlineLimit: 0` when images must remain emitted files instead of being inlined:

```ts
export default defineConfig({
	build: {
		assetsInlineLimit: 0,
	},
});
```

## Public files

The maintained `basic` template demonstrates fixed root-relative assets with files under `public`.
Its route fetches `public/users.json` through `/users.json`, and its document refers to `public/favicon.ico` through `/favicon.ico`.

```tsx
<link rel="icon" href="/favicon.ico" />
```

These template files are addressed by their root-relative names rather than imported from the module graph.
Import an asset instead when application code needs the URL produced for a module dependency.

## Start-mode output

The official start-mode templates have no `index.html`.
Import application CSS from `App`, an authored entry, or another module reachable from them.

During start-mode development, the page response inlines CSS reachable from the generated `App` and `Document` roots.
With authored entries, it uses the authored client entry plus `Document` in client mode, or the authored server entry in SSR mode.
The style elements carry Vite development identifiers, and the start-mode patch removes the server-rendered copy when Vite's client-side style takes over.
CSS hot updates continue through Vite without leaving both copies active.

In production, entry CSS is emitted as built assets and the generated handler adds stylesheet links to the document head.
Imported asset URLs point at their built output, including Vite's generated file names.
CSS modules, preprocessors, and URL imports keep their normal Vite syntax in both client and SSR modes.
---

# Head and metadata

Source: https://v2-rebuild--solid-docs-v2.netlify.app/building-apps/head-and-metadata.md

## Installation

```sh
npm i @solidjs/meta
pnpm i @solidjs/meta
yarn add @solidjs/meta
bun i @solidjs/meta
deno add npm:@solidjs/meta
```

Solid Meta 1.x requires compatible Solid 2 RC builds of `solid-js` and `@solidjs/web`, or later versions.
It is an ergonomic component layer over the ambient head registry that Solid ships through `@solidjs/web`.
Applications can use the components for common tags and call [`useHead`](https://v2-rebuild--solid-docs-v2.netlify.app/reference/solid-web/head/use-head) directly for lower-level registration.

## Usage

There is no provider and no other setup.
Render head components anywhere in your component tree:

```tsx
import { Title, Link, Meta } from "@solidjs/meta";

function Home() {
	return (
		<div class="Home">
			<Title>Title of page</Title>
			<Link rel="canonical" href="https://solidjs.com/" />
			<Meta name="description" content="A description of this page" />
		</div>
	);
}
```

Head tags follow a few consistent rules:

* **Later wins.**
  Tags deduplicate by identity (each [component's reference page](https://v2-rebuild--solid-docs-v2.netlify.app/reference/solid-meta/title) documents its identity rule); the last-registered tag for an identity is the one in the document.
* **Disposal restores.**
  When the winning tag's component unmounts, the previous registration for that identity is restored — navigating away from a page undoes its head changes automatically.
* **Reactive.**
  Attribute values and text children can be reactive expressions; updates apply in place without losing the tag's position in the override order.
