Every component accepts a `key` prop that overrides the default identity — use it to make otherwise-distinct tags override each other, or to fork an identity that would otherwise collide:

```tsx
{/* These override each other despite different attributes: */}
<Meta key="social-image" name="twitter:image" content="/twitter.png" />
<Meta key="social-image" property="og:image" content="/og.png" />
```

## Group related tags

Use [`<Head>`](https://v2-rebuild--solid-docs-v2.netlify.app/reference/solid-meta/head) when several tags form one replacement set.
Tags with the same identity coexist inside a group.
A later group replaces the earlier set as one unit, and unmounting the later group restores the earlier set.
Group membership stays reactive as child tags mount and unmount.

This layout provides two default social images:

```tsx
import { Head, Meta } from "@solidjs/meta";

function SocialDefaults() {
	return (
		<Head>
			<Meta property="og:image" content="/default-wide.png" />
			<Meta property="og:image" content="/default-square.png" />
		</Head>
	);
}
```

A page can replace both defaults while it remains mounted:

```tsx
function ProductSocialTags(props: { image: string }) {
	return (
		<Head>
			<Meta property="og:image" content={props.image} />
			<Meta name="twitter:card" content="summary_large_image" />
		</Head>
	);
}
```

An inner `<Head>` starts an independent group.
Render bare Meta components from child components when those tags should join the surrounding group.

## Use the core head registry

Call `useHead` from `@solidjs/web` when a library or application needs descriptor-level control.
Solid Meta uses the same primitive internally.

```tsx
import { useHead } from "@solidjs/web";

function ProductDescription(props: { description: string }) {
	useHead({
		tag: "meta",
		props: {
			name: "description",
			content: () => props.description,
		},
	});

	return null;
}
```

Pass an array to register one replacement group.
Pass a function to make the group's membership reactive.
For common application metadata, prefer Solid Meta components because their props and tag identities are easier to read in JSX.
See [`useHead`](https://v2-rebuild--solid-docs-v2.netlify.app/reference/solid-web/head/use-head) and [`HeadTag`](https://v2-rebuild--solid-docs-v2.netlify.app/reference/solid-web/head/head-tag) for the descriptor contract.

## Server rendering

Server rendering requires no wiring.
Render your document with Solid and the head manages itself:

```tsx
import { renderToStream } from "@solidjs/web";
import App from "./App";

// ... within the context of a request ...
renderToStream(() => <App />).pipe(res);
```

Winning tags are spliced into `<head>` on the first flush (`<base>` and `<meta charset>` go right after `<head>` opens; resource links go early).
Tags registered under a suspense boundary that completes later stream to the client as patches that apply when the boundary reveals.
If you assemble the document yourself, use the `onHead` render option to receive the head markup instead.

On the client, hydration adopts server-rendered head tags in place — there is no removal/re-insertion flicker.

:::note[Static shell tags]
A static `<title>` in your server shell acts as the fallback when no `<Title>` is mounted. Don't hardcode other tags that Solid Meta should manage — the registry leaves foreign head tags alone, so a hardcoded `<meta name="description">` would coexist with a rendered one.
:::
---

# Server functions

Source: https://v2-rebuild--solid-docs-v2.netlify.app/building-apps/server-functions.md

Server functions let browser code call functions whose implementations stay in the server build.
Enable the transform with the `serverFunctions` option in start mode:

```ts
import { defineConfig } from "vite";
import solid from "@solidjs/vite-plugin";

export default defineConfig({
	plugins: [
		solid({
			start: true,
			ssr: true,
			serverFunctions: true,
		}),
	],
});
```

The official `solid-v2/fullstack` and `solid-v2/fullstack-tanstack` templates use this configuration.

## The `use server` directive

A function-level directive extracts that function into the server build:

```ts
export async function findUser(id: string) {
	"use server";
	return database.users.find(id);
}
```

A module-level directive turns every exported function in that module into a server function:

```ts
"use server";

export async function listUsers() {
	return database.users.all();
}

export async function countUsers() {
	return database.users.count();
}
```

Every export from a module-level server file must be a function.
Function-level server functions may use module-scope bindings, but they cannot close over component or other local scope.
Pass untrusted call data as arguments.
Read trusted identity and other request context from `getRequestEvent()` instead of accepting credentials from the caller.

The transform replaces the implementation with a fetch-backed reference in the client build.
The server build registers the implementation under a stable identifier.
Calling the reference during server rendering runs the implementation in process instead of making an HTTP request.

Treat every argument as untrusted input because the HTTP boundary does not enforce TypeScript types.
Validate authorization and input inside the server function so the checks run for both HTTP dispatch and in-process calls.

## Reads and writes

Server functions use `POST` by default.
Declare an HTTP `GET` read with the core `GET` wrapper:

```ts
import { GET } from "@solidjs/web/server-functions";

export const getUser = GET(async (id: string) => {
	"use server";
	return database.users.find(id);
});
```

The client places a `GET` function's arguments in the URL.
The server accepts `GET` only for functions declared with `GET`, while `POST` remains available.
Use `GET` only for safe, idempotent reads.
Do not pass secrets or sensitive payloads because URLs can appear in browser history, server logs, and cache keys.
Set response cache headers when HTTP caches may store the result.

Leave writes on the default `POST` transport:

The mutation examples assume authentication middleware has populated typed `userId` and `isAdmin` fields on `event.locals`.
See [Sessions and authentication](https://v2-rebuild--solid-docs-v2.netlify.app/building-apps/sessions-and-auth) for the request-scoped pattern.

```ts
import { getRequestEvent, redirect } from "@solidjs/web";
import { z } from "zod";

const UserName = z.string().trim().min(1).max(100);

export async function updateCurrentUser(form: FormData) {
	"use server";
	const event = getRequestEvent();
	const userId = event?.locals.userId;
	if (!userId) throw redirect("/sign-in");

	const name = UserName.parse(form.get("name"));
	await database.users.update(userId, { name });
}
```

A server function is a transport primitive and does not require a router or data cache.

## The request event

Start mode creates one request event for each dispatched request.
Read it with `getRequestEvent()`:

```ts
import { getRequestEvent } from "@solidjs/web";

export async function currentUserId() {
	"use server";
	const event = getRequestEvent();
	if (!event) throw new Error("Missing request event");

	const authorization = event.request.headers.get("authorization");
	event.locals.authorization = authorization;
	return event.locals.userId;
}
```

The event contains the incoming web `Request`, a mutable `locals` bag, and a response-head stub.
Middleware and server functions handling the same request see the same `locals`.
Append outgoing cookies and other response metadata before the response head commits:

```ts
import {
	getRequestEvent,
	type RequestEvent,
	type ResponseStub,
} from "@solidjs/web";

const event = getRequestEvent() as RequestEvent & { response: ResponseStub };
event.response.headers.append("set-cookie", serializedCookie);
```

The handler folds the response stub into the outgoing response after dispatch.

## Values, errors, and responses

Return serializable values for ordinary results.
The client reference resolves to the decoded value.

Use `respond` when a value also needs status, headers, or revalidation metadata:

```ts
import { getRequestEvent, respond } from "@solidjs/web";
import { z } from "zod";

const CreateUser = z.object({
	name: z.string().trim().min(1).max(100),
	email: z.string().email(),
});

export async function createUser(input: unknown) {
	"use server";
	const event = getRequestEvent();
	if (!event?.locals.isAdmin) {
		return respond({ error: "Forbidden" }, { status: 403 });
	}

	const user = await database.users.create(CreateUser.parse(input));
	return respond(user, {
		status: 201,
		headers: { "x-created-user": user.id },
	});
}
```

Scripted callers receive the carried value, while the transport forwards the response metadata.
`redirect()` and `reload()` return standard `Response` objects carrying `Location` or revalidation headers.
Returning or throwing those responses preserves their control-flow metadata for an integration to interpret.
The core transport does not choose how an application navigates.

A plain thrown error reaches the client with its details during development.
Production builds replace unbranded thrown values with a generic `Internal Server Error`.
Use `respond()` for an intentional structured failure, or `markSafeError()` only when the error details are safe to expose.

## Optional Solid Router integration

Solid Router can add caching, submissions, revalidation, forms, and single-flight data to server functions.
These wrappers are optional:

```ts
import { action, query } from "@solidjs/router";
import { getRequestEvent, redirect } from "@solidjs/web";
import { z } from "zod";

const UserName = z.string().trim().min(1).max(100);

export const getUsers = query(async () => {
	"use server";
	return database.users.all();
}, "users");

export const renameCurrentUser = action(async (form: FormData) => {
	"use server";
	const event = getRequestEvent();
	const userId = event?.locals.userId;
	if (!userId) throw redirect("/sign-in");

	await database.users.update(userId, {
		name: UserName.parse(form.get("name")),
	});
});
```

Place the `"use server"` directive inside the callback passed to `query()` or `action()`.

`query()` caches reads by its name and arguments and declares a wrapped server function as an HTTP `GET`.
`action()` tracks mutation submissions and applies response metadata to the router's query cache.
When the router registers its single-flight integration, an action response can carry refreshed route data with the mutation result.
The server-function runtime treats that data as an opaque payload, so other routers and caches can integrate through the same transport hooks.

:::note[API status]
Server functions and the start-mode serving layer are part of the Solid 2.0 RC surface.
The low-level handler hooks, codec extensions, and single-flight integration seams are intended for router and framework integrations and may still change before a stable release.
:::
---

# Sessions and auth

Source: https://v2-rebuild--solid-docs-v2.netlify.app/building-apps/sessions-and-auth.md

Solid supplies the HTTP exchange that session and authentication code can use.
It does not supply a built-in session store or authentication framework.

## What the platform supplies

Code running under start mode can read the current request event with `getRequestEvent()`.
The event exposes:

* `request`, including its `Cookie`, `Authorization`, and other request headers.
* `locals`, where middleware can place request-scoped state such as an authenticated user.
* `response`, whose headers collect outgoing `Set-Cookie` values before the response head commits.

`@solidjs/web` also exports `parseCookieHeader()` and `serializeCookie()` for unsigned cookie encoding.
Those functions do not sign, encrypt, rotate, persist, or revoke session data.

## What a session library supplies

A cookie or session library defines the application-level protocol.
Depending on the library, that can include:

* Signing or encryption.
* Secret rotation.
* Expiration and renewal rules.
* Storage-backed session identifiers.
* Cookie serialization options.

The official `solid-v2/fullstack` templates compose `@remix-run/cookie` with the request event.
Their session cookie is signed and tamper-evident, but it is not encrypted.
Do not place secrets in a client-readable cookie payload.

## A signed cookie session

The following shape follows the fullstack template:

```ts
import { createCookie } from "@remix-run/cookie";
import {
	getRequestEvent,
	type RequestEvent,
	type ResponseStub,
} from "@solidjs/web";
import { env } from "virtual:env/server";

interface SessionData {
	userId?: string;
}

const maxAge = 60 * 60 * 24 * 7;

const sessionCookie = createCookie("session", {
	secrets: env.SESSION_SECRET,
	httpOnly: true,
	secure: true,
	sameSite: "Lax",
	maxAge,
});

function event(): RequestEvent & { response: ResponseStub } {
	const event = getRequestEvent();
	if (!event) throw new Error("Missing request event");
	return event as RequestEvent & { response: ResponseStub };
}

export async function getSession(): Promise<SessionData | null> {
	const raw = await sessionCookie.parse(event().request.headers.get("cookie"));
	if (!raw) return null;

	try {
		const { data, exp } = JSON.parse(raw);
		return Date.now() < exp * 1000 ? data : null;
	} catch {
		return null;
	}
}

export async function setSession(data: SessionData): Promise<void> {
	const exp = Math.floor(Date.now() / 1000) + maxAge;
	event().response.headers.append(
		"set-cookie",
		await sessionCookie.serialize(
			JSON.stringify({
				data,
				exp,
			})
		)
	);
}

export async function clearSession(): Promise<void> {
	event().response.headers.append(
		"set-cookie",
		await sessionCookie.serialize("", { maxAge: 0 })
	);
}
```

Set cookie attributes explicitly because library defaults may not match the application's security requirements.
Use a server-only environment source for signing secrets.

The first secret signs new cookies, and every listed secret can verify existing cookies.
Prepend a new secret during rotation, then remove the old secret after existing cookies have expired.
The example stores its own expiry timestamp because browser `Max-Age` alone does not stop a client from replaying an old cookie.

Cookie reads and writes are separate views.
`getSession()` reads the `Cookie` header that arrived with the request.
Calling `setSession()` does not change that incoming header during the same request.

## Authorize on the server

Authentication identifies the caller.
Authorization decides whether that caller may perform an operation.
Both decisions belong in server code.

```ts
import { redirect } from "@solidjs/web";
import { z } from "zod";

const UserName = z.string().trim().min(1).max(100);

export async function renameCurrentUser(form: FormData) {
	"use server";
	const session = await getSession();
	if (!session?.userId) throw redirect("/sign-in");

	const name = UserName.parse(form.get("name"));
	await database.users.rename(session.userId, name);
}
```

Hiding a control in the browser does not authorize the corresponding server function or API route.
Check the session or other credentials again at every protected server entry point, then authorize access to the specific record.
Do not accept a user ID from the browser when the operation should target the signed-in user.

Middleware can authenticate once and place the result on `event.locals`.
Server functions, API handlers, and the page render can then read the same request-scoped value.
Use TypeScript module augmentation on `RequestEventLocals` when the application needs a precise type for those fields.

## Response behavior

Session writes append `Set-Cookie` to the event response stub.
Start mode folds those cookies onto page responses, middleware responses, API responses, and server-function responses.
A session write followed by a thrown `redirect()` keeps both the cookie and redirect metadata.

Write cookies before the response head commits.
During streaming server rendering, the response head commits when the shell flushes.
A late response-header write throws in development and reports an error without changing the response in production.

:::note[Choose the session architecture]
Cookie-backed sessions keep their payload in the browser and are limited by cookie size.
Storage-backed sessions put only an opaque identifier in the cookie and keep revocable data in a database, key-value store, or other server storage.
Solid supplies the same request and response seam for either architecture.
:::
---

# Environment

Source: https://v2-rebuild--solid-docs-v2.netlify.app/building-apps/environment.md

Start mode can validate environment variables and expose their typed outputs through separate server and client modules.
This environment layer belongs to start mode and is not enabled by `ssr: true` alone.

## Define the schema

Add `env.ts` or `env.js` at the Vite project root.
Default-export optional `server` and `client` maps whose values implement Standard Schema.
The official `solid-v2/fullstack` template uses Zod:

```ts
import { z } from "zod";

const signingKeys = z.preprocess(
	(value) =>
		typeof value === "string"
			? value.split(",").map((key) => key.trim())
			: value,
	z.array(z.string().min(32)).min(1)
);

export default {
	server: {
		SESSION_SECRET: signingKeys,
	},
	client: {
		VITE_APP_NAME: z.string().min(1).default("Solid App"),
	},
};
```

The `SESSION_SECRET` input is a comma-separated list.
The schema validates every signing key after splitting the input, so a short or empty rotation key fails validation.

The validators may come from different Standard Schema libraries because the plugin reads the shared Standard Schema interface.
Only keys declared by the schema appear in the virtual modules.

Start mode probes the root schema automatically.
The `start.env` option can require the convention, select another schema inside the Vite root, or disable probing:

```ts
solid({
	start: {
		env: "./config/env.ts",
	},
});
```

Use `env: true` to report an error when neither root schema exists.
Use `env: false` to disable the environment layer even when a conventional schema exists.

## Generated types

The plugin writes `solid-env.d.ts` next to the schema when development or a build starts.
The declarations infer each value from the Standard Schema output type of its validator.
Keep both the schema and generated declaration in the TypeScript project.

Both virtual modules provide a named and a default `env` export:

```ts
import { env } from "virtual:env/client";
```

Do not edit `solid-env.d.ts` because the plugin regenerates it from the schema.

## Client environment

`virtual:env/client` contains only values declared in the schema's `client` map.
Each client key must start with Vite's public environment prefix, which is `VITE_` unless `envPrefix` changes it.
The prefix requirement is enforced when the schema is loaded.

Client values are validated during development and production builds.
The validated outputs are serialized into the browser bundle as plain JSON, so defaults and coercions run before serialization.
The validator library is not included with those generated values.
A client validation failure stops the build.
During development, a validation failure is reported through Vite's error response or overlay.

These values are public because anyone can inspect a browser bundle.
Do not place credentials or other secrets in the `client` map, regardless of how the application uses them.
The client module can also be imported by isomorphic code because it contains only public values.

## Server environment

`virtual:env/server` exposes the schema's server values and its public client values to server module graphs.
Server values are read from `process.env` and validated when the server bundle starts.
They are not serialized into the server bundle, so a deployment platform can inject or rotate them without rebuilding.

```ts
import { env } from "virtual:env/server";

const signingKeys = env.SESSION_SECRET;
```

Import this module from middleware, a `"use server"` module, an authored server entry, or another server-only module.
Importing it from the application root or another client module graph is a build error that names the importer.
An SSR component is not automatically server-only because its module can also be included in the hydration bundle.

The plugin validates server values during development because the development server is the running server.
During a production build, a missing or invalid server value produces a warning and defers the failure to server boot.
This lets build systems create artifacts before deployment-only secrets exist.

In client mode, only the client map is available to the static browser graph.
The server virtual module remains unavailable to client code.

## Environment file loading

When environment-file loading is enabled, the layer loads `.env`, `.env.local`, `.env.[mode]`, and `.env.[mode].local` from Vite's configured environment directory.
It folds those file-loaded values into `process.env` during development and builds.
Values already present in the process take precedence over values loaded from files.
Application server libraries can therefore read the same file-loaded values from `process.env` without `loadEnv` setup in `vite.config.ts`.
When a built server handler is retained, `vite preview` also folds the selected files before that module performs its boot validation.

The production client build also performs a heuristic leak scan over non-vendor chunks.
It checks exact quoted copies of validated server strings that are at least eight characters long and are not also client values.
Shorter values, non-string values, and values unavailable on the build machine are outside that scan.
The scan is a secondary check and does not make a client value secret.
The server/client schema split and module-graph boundaries remain the controls that keep server data out of browser code.

## Server-only and client-only modules

`@solidjs/vite-plugin` enables the `server-only` and `client-only` marker modules even when start mode and server functions are off.
Import a marker for its side effect at the top of a module:

```ts
import "server-only";

export function readFromDatabase() {
	// Server implementation.
}
```

If this module enters a client graph, resolution fails and reports the importing file.
In a server graph, the marker resolves to an empty module.

`client-only` enforces the reverse rule:

```ts
import "client-only";

export function readBrowserStorage() {
	return localStorage.getItem("preference");
}
```

If this module enters an SSR graph, the build fails.
In a client graph, the marker resolves to an empty module.

The markers assert where a module may be bundled.
They do not move code between environments, create a runtime condition, or hide values that have already been copied into client code.
The marker must be imported by the module that needs the assertion.
Keep browser modules out of server import graphs, and keep secrets in modules reached only from server entries, middleware, or server functions.

Add the plugin's boundary declarations when TypeScript does not already include them:

```ts
/// <reference types="@solidjs/vite-plugin/boundary-modules" />
```
---

# Middleware and API routes

Source: https://v2-rebuild--solid-docs-v2.netlify.app/building-apps/middleware-and-api-routes.md

Start mode accepts fetch-style middleware that runs before its request handler.
Configure a server-only middleware module with `start.middleware`:

```ts
import { defineConfig } from "vite";
import solid from "@solidjs/vite-plugin";

export default defineConfig({
	plugins: [
		solid({
			start: {
				middleware: "./src/middleware.ts",
			},
			ssr: true,
		}),
	],
});
```

The module must default-export one middleware function or an array of middleware functions.

## Middleware contract

Each function receives a web `Request` and a `next` continuation:

```ts
type Middleware = (
	request: Request,
	next: (request?: Request) => Promise<Response>
) => Response | Promise<Response>;
```

Call `next()` to advance to the next middleware.
Pass a `Request` to `next(request)` to replace the request for the remaining chain.
Return a `Response` without calling `next()` to stop the chain.
Do not call `next()` more than once from the same middleware invocation.

Middleware runs in array order before the request handler and unwinds in reverse order:

```ts
import { getRequestEvent } from "@solidjs/web";

async function authenticate(request: Request, next: () => Promise<Response>) {
	const event = getRequestEvent()!;
	event.locals.user = await readUser(request);

	const response = await next();
	response.headers.set("x-authenticated", String(Boolean(event.locals.user)));
	return response;
}

async function catchErrors(_request: Request, next: () => Promise<Response>) {
	try {
		return await next();
	} catch (error) {
		console.error(error);
		return Response.json({ error: "Internal Server Error" }, { status: 500 });
	}
}

export default [catchErrors, authenticate];
```

Return details only for errors that the application explicitly marks as safe for clients.
Do not expose arbitrary exception messages because they can contain database, filesystem, token, or internal-state details.

Nothing is written to the network until the outermost middleware returns.
Middleware can therefore change headers on the returned `Response` after `await next()`, including responses with streamed bodies.

The chain runs inside the request-event scope.
`getRequestEvent()` exposes the same `locals` and response stub that the page render or server function sees.
The chain fronts page rendering, server-function dispatch, and other requests handled by configured middleware in development, preview, and production.

## API routes in the fullstack template

API route discovery is supplied by the `filesystem-routing` integration used in the official fullstack templates.
It is not part of the core middleware primitive.

Enable HTTP method discovery and place its dispatcher in the middleware chain:

```ts
// vite.config.ts
import { fileRoutes } from "filesystem-routing/vite";

export default defineConfig({
	plugins: [
		solid({
			start: { middleware: "./src/middleware.ts" },
			ssr: true,
		}),
		fileRoutes({ httpMethods: true }),
	],
});
```

```ts
// src/middleware.ts
import { createAPIHandler } from "filesystem-routing/api";
import routes from "virtual:file-routes";

export default [createAPIHandler(routes)];
```

Export an uppercase HTTP method from a route module:

```ts
// src/routes/api/users.ts
import type { APIHandler } from "filesystem-routing/api";

export const GET: APIHandler = ({ request, params }) => {
	return Response.json({
		path: new URL(request.url).pathname,
		params,
	});
};
```

A module with method exports and no default component is an API-only route.
A module may also contain a page component and a `GET` handler.

## API handler contract

The dispatcher writes matched route parameters to the request event and passes that event to the method handler.
Return a `Response` to control the full status, headers, and body.
A returned string becomes a text `Response`, and another returned value becomes a JSON `Response`.

Requests with no matching method continue to the next middleware.
`HEAD` uses a route's `HEAD` handler when present and otherwise falls back to `GET`.
A `GET` handler in a page module may return `undefined` to let the page render.
An API-only `GET` handler that returns `undefined` produces a `404` response.
Any other matched method that returns `undefined` throws because the handler did not provide a response.

API handlers run under the same request event as the surrounding middleware.
They can read authenticated state from `event.locals` and append cookies to `event.response.headers`.
Keep authorization checks in the handler or in middleware that is guaranteed to run before it.

:::note[Integration boundary]
The method-export convention and route matching belong to `filesystem-routing`.
The stable contract exposed by Solid's server runtime is the fetch-style `Request` and `Response` middleware shape.
:::
---

# Deployment

Source: https://v2-rebuild--solid-docs-v2.netlify.app/building-apps/deployment.md

Start mode produces a server handler with a web-standard request and response interface.
The built module and static assets still require host-compatible module resolution, dependencies, environment access, and asset configuration.
The official `solid-v2/fullstack` and `solid-v2/fullstack-tanstack` templates demonstrate the supported contract.

## Build output

Run the configured Vite build:

```bash
pnpm build
```

With `start` and `ssr: true`, one build produces:

* `dist/client`, containing browser JavaScript, CSS, images, and other static assets.
* `dist/server/server.js`, containing the server entry and its exported request handler.

When server functions are enabled, the server bundle also dispatches their configured endpoint.
The default endpoint is `/_server`.

Without `ssr: true`, start mode prerenders a static document shell to `dist/client/index.html`.
It removes `dist/server` when the application has no server functions.
If server functions remain enabled, deploy `dist/client` for pages and keep `dist/server` for the function endpoint.

## The request handler

The built server entry exports the handler in two forms:

```ts
import app, { handleRequest } from "./dist/server/server.js";

const response = await handleRequest(request);
const sameResponse = await app.fetch(request);
```

`handleRequest(Request)` is the direct Solid API.
The default export follows the Fetchable module convention used by Workers, Nitro, Netlify Functions, Bun, and `deno serve`:

```ts
export default {
	fetch(request: Request) {
		return handleRequest(request);
	},
};
```

The wrapper intentionally accepts only the request.
Some hosts call `fetch` with additional environment or execution-context arguments, which are not Solid handler options.

The handler creates the request event, runs configured middleware, dispatches the server-function endpoint when enabled, renders pages, and commits response metadata.
It also resolves built client entry and stylesheet URLs through the client build manifest.

Serve files from `dist/client` before passing unmatched requests to `handleRequest`.
Static assets should not enter the page renderer.
Preserve the request URL, method, headers, and non-`GET` or non-`HEAD` body when adapting a host request.
Preserve the response status, headers, separate `Set-Cookie` values, and streamed body when adapting the result.

## Node

The fullstack templates include `server.js` as the verified Node adapter example.
It converts `node:http` requests to web `Request` objects, serves `dist/client`, calls `handleRequest`, and streams the resulting response.
The template's start script runs that file:

```json
{
	"scripts": {
		"start": "node --env-file-if-exists=.env server.js"
	}
}
```

Use the template adapter as a reference when integrating another Node server.
The bridge must use a streamed request body for methods other than `GET` and `HEAD`.
It must forward multiple `Set-Cookie` headers as separate values instead of joining them with commas.

## Preview the production artifact

The start-mode integration configures `vite preview` to serve `dist/client` and dispatch remaining requests through the built handler:

```bash
pnpm build
pnpm exec vite preview
```

The official fullstack templates name the Vite preview script `serve` and reserve `start` for the included Node server:

```bash
pnpm build
pnpm serve
```

Preview verifies the built request handler and static assets together.
It does not replace the target host's deployment test.

## Provider integrations

Provider Vite plugins can add platform development features and prepare deployment output.
The normal `ssr` environment exposes the default Fetchable handler as its `index` service entry in development and production.
A provider plugin can adopt that environment, supply its runtime and build orchestration, and use the Solid entry without a custom source file or explicit Rollup input.

The integrations below adopt the normal `ssr` environment.
Use `start.external` instead when a custom host controls a differently named or independently configured server environment.
External mode leaves the server build and development HTTP serving to that host while Solid continues to provide its generated entries, manifest, and virtual request handler.

### Netlify

The Netlify Vite plugin consumes Solid's normal `ssr` build and turns its Fetchable server entry into a streaming Netlify Function.

```sh
npm i @netlify/vite-plugin -D
pnpm i @netlify/vite-plugin -D
yarn add @netlify/vite-plugin -D
bun i @netlify/vite-plugin -d
deno add npm:@netlify/vite-plugin -D
```

Add the Netlify plugin after `solid()` and enable its build support:

```ts title="vite.config.ts"
import netlify from "@netlify/vite-plugin";
import { defineConfig } from "vite";
import solid from "@solidjs/vite-plugin";

export default defineConfig({
	plugins: [
		solid({
			start: true,
			ssr: true,
		}),
		netlify({
			build: {
				enabled: true,
			},
		}),
	],
});
```

Keep the normal Solid server build enabled so Netlify can consume its `ssr` environment.

Set the direct-plugin build defaults explicitly:

```toml title="netlify.toml"
[build]
command = "pnpm build"
publish = "dist/client"
```

The Netlify plugin generates the catch-all function, gives static files precedence, and preserves streaming responses.
It also emulates Netlify platform features during `vite dev`.
No handwritten Netlify Function is required.

### Nitro

[Nitro v3](https://nitro.build/) adopts Solid's `ssr` environment so its presets, route rules, tasks, and runtime features apply to the Solid handler.

```sh
npm i nitro
pnpm i nitro
yarn add nitro
bun i nitro
deno add npm:nitro
```

Add `nitro()` after `solid()`:

```tsx title="vite.config.ts"
import { nitro } from "nitro/vite";
import { defineConfig } from "vite";
import solid from "@solidjs/vite-plugin";

export default defineConfig({
	plugins: [
		solid({
			start: true,
			ssr: true,
		}),
		nitro({ serverEntry: false }),
	],
});
```

Nitro discovers the Fetchable handler through the environment's `index` service entry.
No custom server entry or Rollup input is required.

Use the top-level `nitro` property for deployment presets, prerendering, tasks, WebSockets, and other Nitro options. See the [Nitro configuration reference](https://nitro.build/config).

### Cloudflare Workers

The [Cloudflare Vite plugin](https://developers.cloudflare.com/workers/vite-plugin/) runs the server build in the Workers runtime during development and prepares it for deployment to Cloudflare.

```sh
npm i @cloudflare/vite-plugin wrangler -D
pnpm i @cloudflare/vite-plugin wrangler -D
yarn add @cloudflare/vite-plugin wrangler -D
bun i @cloudflare/vite-plugin wrangler -d
deno add npm:@cloudflare/vite-plugin wrangler -D
```

Map the Worker to Solid's `ssr` environment:

```tsx title="vite.config.ts"
import { cloudflare } from "@cloudflare/vite-plugin";
import { defineConfig } from "vite";
import solid from "@solidjs/vite-plugin";

export default defineConfig({
	plugins: [
		cloudflare({ viteEnvironment: { name: "ssr" } }),
		solid({ start: true, ssr: true }),
	],
});
```

Cloudflare comes before `solid()` in the plugin list, matching Cloudflare's framework-integration order.
It associates the Worker with the `ssr` environment before Solid supplies the application entry.

Point the Worker at Solid's generated virtual handler in `wrangler.jsonc`:

```jsonc
{
	"$schema": "./node_modules/wrangler/config-schema.json",
	"name": "solid-2",
	"main": "virtual:solid-ssr-handler",
	"compatibility_date": "2026-07-22",
	"compatibility_flags": ["nodejs_compat"],
	"assets": {
		"directory": "./dist/client",
		"binding": "ASSETS",
	},
	"observability": {
		"enabled": true,
	},
}
```

The `viteEnvironment` option merges the Workers runtime configuration with Solid's server environment.
The Cloudflare plugin resolves the environment's Fetchable `index` entry and runs it in workerd.
No custom Worker source entry is required.
Follow the Cloudflare guide to add any platform bindings.

## Other Fetch runtimes

When a runtime accepts a default Fetchable module, point it at `dist/server/server.js` and configure its static asset service for `dist/client`.
For example, Bun and `deno serve` can start modules that default-export an object with a `fetch` method.
The runtime still needs the server bundle's production dependencies and environment variables.
---

# Routing overview

Source: https://v2-rebuild--solid-docs-v2.netlify.app/routing/overview.md

Routing is an application integration rather than a requirement of Solid.
A router integration owns URL matching, navigation, and route rendering.
Route-level data can use the router's data APIs or a separate cache.
The application renders the router as part of the component tree.

## Mount the router in `App`

The `@solidjs/vite-plugin` start mode uses `src/App.tsx` as the root component for its generated entries.
With server-side rendering (SSR) enabled, its generated server and client entries both render `App` inside `src/Document.tsx`.
Mount the router or its provider in `App` so both entries use the same router root.
Site-wide providers can wrap the router, and shared layouts can live in the router's root route or inside its provider.

## Choose how routes are defined

The platform does not prescribe route definitions or file naming.
A router can consume an in-memory route tree, its own generated route tree, or a manifest from a file-system routing plugin.

The `filesystem-routing` Vite plugin exposes its manifest through `virtual:file-routes`.
Solid Router can convert the manifest's `pageRoutes` export into route definitions with `fileRoutes` from `@solidjs/router/fs`.
This import and adapter are Solid Router conventions, not requirements for other routers.

TanStack Router uses `@tanstack/router-plugin` to generate its own typed route tree from `src/routes`.
An application can still use `virtual:file-routes` for HTTP handlers in another directory, as the Router + Query template does for `src/api`.

## Connect routing to requests

During SSR, a router needs the request URL and any request-scoped data required by its loaders.
The start mode request handler creates a `RequestEvent` for each web `Request` and runs the render inside that event's scope.

Solid Router reads the request URL from that event when it creates its server integration.
That behavior belongs to Solid Router.

Routers that need an asynchronous preparation step can use the platform's `start.setup` option.
The configured server-only module runs when the middleware chain reaches the page render and before `renderToStream`.
It can create request-local router and cache instances, load the matched routes, and return a component to render in place of `App`.
The hook runs once each time a generated server entry renders an SSR request and is ignored when SSR is disabled.
An authored server entry already owns the render function and must perform the same preparation itself.

The separate `start.middleware` option installs fetch-style middleware in front of page renders, server-function calls, and other requests dispatched by the handler.
The middleware chain and `start.setup` share the same request event, including values stored in `event.locals`.

## Integrate single-flight mutations

Single-flight combines a mutation result and refreshed page data in one server-function response.
The platform transport supplies two router-neutral extension points:

* Registering a client consumer with `subscribeFlightData` adds the single-flight request header to non-GET server-function calls.
* A server `collectFlightData` hook receives the target URL, revalidation keys, and request headers with mutation cookies already folded in.

The server-function runtime awaits the consumer before resolving the call with the mutation value.
The integration decides how to match the target URL, rerun relevant loaders or preloads, produce a serializable payload, and apply it on the client.
`serverFunctions.configure` provides a server-only module that the generated handler imports before dispatch in development and production.

Solid Router supplies `createFlightDataCollector` from `@solidjs/router/server`.
It consumes the router's route tree, base, and root preload, then collects matched `query` results for the target URL.
On the client, Solid Router enables single-flight by default, registers the mounted router, and installs its flight consumer when the first router action is created.
The router and action integrations use a rendezvous so either can load first.
The consumer applies response metadata and seeds Solid Router's `query` cache from the payload.

Other routers provide their own collection and cache-hydration policy.
The [TanStack Router integration](https://v2-rebuild--solid-docs-v2.netlify.app/routing/tanstack) uses a dehydrated TanStack Query cache as the flight payload.
---

# Solid Router

Source: https://v2-rebuild--solid-docs-v2.netlify.app/routing/solid-router.md

Solid publishes Solid Router as `@solidjs/router`.
Add the optional package when a Solid application needs URL matching, navigation, nested layouts, or route-aware data loading.
Solid applications can use other routers.

Solid Router 2 defines routes as configuration objects.
`createRouter` turns the route tree into a provider component, a typed path builder, and a URL matcher.
Components read the active routing session through primitives such as `useLocation`, `useParams`, and `useNavigate`.

## Start here

1. [Install and create a router](https://v2-rebuild--solid-docs-v2.netlify.app/routing/solid-router/setup) to mount a minimal application.
2. [Define routes](https://v2-rebuild--solid-docs-v2.netlify.app/routing/solid-router/route-definitions) with static, dynamic, optional, wildcard, lazy, or file-system-backed paths.
3. [Build nested routes and layouts](https://v2-rebuild--solid-docs-v2.netlify.app/routing/solid-router/nested-routes) with `children` and `props.children`.
4. [Navigate with typed paths](https://v2-rebuild--solid-docs-v2.netlify.app/routing/solid-router/navigation) through anchors, `Router.paths`, and routing primitives.
5. [Preload and mutate data](https://v2-rebuild--solid-docs-v2.netlify.app/routing/solid-router/data) with route preloads, `query`, and `action`.
6. [Connect server rendering and hydration](https://v2-rebuild--solid-docs-v2.netlify.app/routing/solid-router/server-rendering) when the application renders on the server or uses server functions.

## Package entries

* `@solidjs/router` contains the router factory, route and navigation primitives, history adapters, queries, and actions.
* `@solidjs/router/fs` converts a `file-routes` manifest into route definitions.
* `@solidjs/router/server` provides the single-flight data collector for a server-function handler.

Use the [Solid Router API reference](https://v2-rebuild--solid-docs-v2.netlify.app/reference/solid-router) for signatures and option details.
---

# Setup

Source: https://v2-rebuild--solid-docs-v2.netlify.app/routing/solid-router/setup.md

Install Solid Router in a Solid 2 application:

```sh
pnpm add @solidjs/router@next
```

Define the route tree and create the router at module scope:

```tsx
// src/router.ts
import { lazy } from "solid-js";
import { createRouter } from "@solidjs/router";

export const Router = createRouter({
	routes: [
		{ path: "/", component: lazy(() => import("./pages/Home")) },
		{ path: "/about", component: lazy(() => import("./pages/About")) },
		{ path: "*404", component: lazy(() => import("./pages/NotFound")) },
	],
});

export const { paths } = Router;
```

`createRouter` returns both the provider component and the application-wide static routing instance.
The instance members `routes`, `config`, `paths`, and `match()` do not represent one visitor's current location.
Session-specific state comes from routing primitives inside the provider.

Mount the instance at the application root:

```tsx
// src/App.tsx
import { Router } from "./router";

export default function App() {
	return (
		<Router>
			{(props) => (
				<>
					<header>
						<a href={Router.paths()}>Home</a>
						<a href={Router.paths.about}>About</a>
					</header>
					<main>{props.children}</main>
				</>
			)}
		</Router>
	);
}
```

The render-prop child is the root layout.
The root layout remains mounted while matched route components render through `props.children`.
If the router config has a top-level `preload`, its return value is available as `props.data`.
In start mode, the generated client and server entries render `src/App.tsx`.

### Mount without start mode

For a transform-only Vite application, render the same application component from a client entry:

```tsx
// src/index.tsx
import { render } from "@solidjs/web";
import App from "./App";

render(() => <App />, document.getElementById("app")!);
```

## Configure the router

`createRouter` accepts these application-level options:

* `routes` is the immutable route tree used for matching and type inference.
* `base` prefixes matching and generated paths.
* `preload` loads root-layout data once per mount or server request.
* `history` selects a history adapter.
* `preloadLinks` controls route code and data preloading from link hover, focus, and touch.
* `explicitLinks` limits delegated routing to anchors with a `link` attribute.
* `singleFlight` controls the server-function data consumer and defaults to `true`.
* `actionBase` sets the server-action URL prefix and defaults to `/_server`.
* `scrollRestoration` controls explicit back and forward scroll restoration.
* `transformUrl` rewrites pathnames before matching.

The router uses browser history by default on the client.
The router uses the current request URL by default on the server.
Pass `hashHistory()` or `memoryHistory()` through `history` for other client-side environments.

```tsx
import { createRouter, hashHistory } from "@solidjs/router";

export const Router = createRouter({
	routes,
	history: hashHistory(),
	base: "/app",
});
```

Solid Router does not support nested routers.
Compose one route tree from extracted route arrays or [lazy route subtrees](https://v2-rebuild--solid-docs-v2.netlify.app/routing/solid-router/route-definitions#load-a-route-subtree-lazily).

See [`createRouter`](https://v2-rebuild--solid-docs-v2.netlify.app/reference/solid-router/router-factory#createrouter) for the full signature.
---

# Route definitions

Source: https://v2-rebuild--solid-docs-v2.netlify.app/routing/solid-router/route-definitions.md

A route definition maps a path pattern to a component and route behavior.

```tsx
import { defineRoutes } from "@solidjs/router";

export const routes = defineRoutes([
	{ path: "/", component: Home },
	{ path: "/about", component: About },
	{ path: "*404", component: NotFound },
]);
```

Use `defineRoutes` for an extracted route array.
The `const` type parameter preserves path literals that TypeScript would otherwise widen.
`createRouter({ routes })` can then infer typed paths from the array.
An inline array passed directly to `createRouter` already receives literal inference.

## Type a route at its definition

`defineRoute` is an identity helper that types a route component and preload from the route's own path pattern.

```tsx
import { defineRoute } from "@solidjs/router";

const storyRoute = defineRoute({
	path: "/stories/:id/:tab?",
	preload: ({ params }) => getStory(params.id),
	component: (props) => (
		<Story story={props.data} id={props.params.id} tab={props.params.tab} />
	),
});
```

Required parameters such as `:id` are typed as `string`.
Optional parameters such as `:tab?` are typed as `string | undefined`.
The return type of `preload` becomes the component's `props.data` type.
Parameters inherited from a parent remain accessible as `string | undefined`.

For a component declared in another module, annotate it with a path witness:

```tsx
import type { RouteComponent } from "@solidjs/router";
import type { Router } from "../router";

const Story: RouteComponent<typeof Router.paths.stories> = (props) => (
	<h1>{props.params.id}</h1>
);
```

`RouteProps<typeof Router.paths.stories>` provides the corresponding props-object type.

## Match path patterns

Choose a path token for each parameter:

* Use `:name` for a required segment.
* Use `:name?` for an optional segment.
* Use `*name` for the remaining path.

A wildcard must be the final segment.

```tsx
const routes = defineRoutes([
	{ path: "/users/:id", component: User },
	{ path: "/docs/:section?", component: Docs },
	{ path: "/files/*path", component: FileViewer },
]);
```

A route can also match any path in an array:

```tsx
{ path: ["/login", "/register"], component: AccountAccess }
```

The same route definition stays mounted when navigation moves between its matching paths.

## Filter parameters

`matchFilters` rejects parameter values that do not satisfy the configured filter.
Use an array, regular expression, or predicate as the filter.

```tsx
import { defineRoute, int } from "@solidjs/router";

const userRoute = defineRoute({
	path: "/users/:id",
	matchFilters: { id: int },
	component: User,
});
```

The built-in `int` filter accepts integer strings at runtime.
The `int` filter also changes the corresponding `Router.paths` argument to `number`.
Route components still receive URL parameters as strings.

## Add route metadata

The `info` property stores application metadata on a route.
Read matched metadata with `useRouteMatches()` or with `Router.match(url)`.

```tsx
const routes = defineRoutes([
	{
		path: "/account",
		component: Account,
		info: { breadcrumb: "Account" },
	},
]);
```

Augment `RouteInfo` to check shared metadata keys throughout the application:

```ts
declare module "@solidjs/router" {
	interface RouteInfo {
		breadcrumb?: string;
	}
}
```

## Load a route subtree lazily

A `children` thunk can load an entire nested route table.
The module may export the route array as `default` or as `routes`.

```tsx
const Router = createRouter({
	routes: [
		{ path: "/", component: Home },
		{
			path: "/admin",
			component: AdminLayout,
			children: () => import("./admin/routes"),
		},
	],
});
```

The thunk runs when matching, navigation, or preloading reaches that subtree.
The router caches the resolved subtree and adds it to the compiled route tree.
TypeScript infers paths through the import's promise type when the imported route array retains literal types.

```tsx
// src/admin/routes.ts
import { defineRoutes } from "@solidjs/router";

export default defineRoutes([
	{ path: "/", component: AdminHome },
	{ path: "/users/:id", component: AdminUser },
]);
```

Use a deterministic thunk such as `() => import("./admin/routes")`.
The resolved subtree is shared and does not switch based on later runtime state.

## Convert a file-system manifest

Enable the `filesystem-routing` Vite plugin to generate `virtual:file-routes`:

```ts
// vite.config.ts
import { defineConfig } from "vite";
import { fileRoutes as fileRoutesPlugin } from "filesystem-routing/vite";
import solid from "@solidjs/vite-plugin";

export default defineConfig({
	plugins: [solid({ start: true }), fileRoutesPlugin()],
});
```

The `@solidjs/router/fs` adapter converts the generated `pageRoutes` export into Solid Router route definitions.

```tsx
import { pageRoutes } from "virtual:file-routes";
import { createRouter } from "@solidjs/router";
import { fileRoutes as routerFileRoutes } from "@solidjs/router/fs";

export const Router = createRouter({
	routes: routerFileRoutes(pageRoutes),
});
```

Each route module exports its component as `default`.
An optional named `route` export can supply:

* `preload`
* `matchFilters`
* `search`
* `info`

```tsx
// routes/blog/[id].tsx
import { int, type RouteProps } from "@solidjs/router";
import { defineFileRoute } from "@solidjs/router/fs";

export const route = defineFileRoute("/blog/:id", {
	matchFilters: { id: int },
	preload: ({ params }) => getPost(params.id),
});

export default function Post(props: RouteProps<typeof route>) {
	return <h1>{props.data.title}</h1>;
}
```

The string passed to `defineFileRoute` is a type witness for the file's path.
The manifest path remains the runtime source of truth.
When the manifest has generated literal types, route paths, filters, and search schemas continue into `Router.paths`.

Code-split manifest components become Solid `lazy` components.
An eagerly delivered component from a manifest built with `codeSplitting: false` is passed through without a `lazy` wrapper.

See the [`@solidjs/router/fs` reference](https://v2-rebuild--solid-docs-v2.netlify.app/reference/solid-router/filesystem) for adapter signatures.
---

# Nested routes and layouts

Source: https://v2-rebuild--solid-docs-v2.netlify.app/routing/solid-router/nested-routes.md

Nested route definitions build a path and component chain from the root to a leaf.
A route with `children` can render a layout around the matched child.

```tsx
import { defineRoutes } from "@solidjs/router";

function AccountLayout(props) {
	return (
		<section>
			<h1>Account</h1>
			<nav>
				<a href="/account">Profile</a>
				<a href="/account/security">Security</a>
			</nav>
			{props.children}
		</section>
	);
}

export const routes = defineRoutes([
	{
		path: "/account",
		component: AccountLayout,
		children: [
			{ path: "/", component: Profile },
			{ path: "/security", component: Security },
		],
	},
]);
```

At `/account/security`, `AccountLayout` renders first.
The layout receives the `Security` route through `props.children`.
Each matched route component receives these props:

* `params`
* `location`
* `data`
* `children`

## Add pathless layouts

A route without `path` adds a component or preload to the match chain without adding a URL segment.

```tsx
const routes = defineRoutes([
	{
		component: AuthenticatedLayout,
		children: [
			{ path: "/dashboard", component: Dashboard },
			{ path: "/settings", component: Settings },
		],
	},
]);
```

Pathless routes keep the open `Params` type because they do not declare parameters of their own.

## Share parent parameters

Child routes inherit parameters from matched parents.

```tsx
const routes = defineRoutes([
	{
		path: "/teams/:teamId",
		component: TeamLayout,
		children: [
			{ path: "/", component: TeamOverview },
			{ path: "/members/:memberId", component: Member },
		],
	},
]);
```

At `/teams/solid/members/42`, the leaf sees both `teamId` and `memberId`.
`useParams()` returns the merged current parameters.
A typed path witness can narrow the known keys:

```tsx
const params = useParams(Router.paths.teams("solid").members);
params.teamId;
params.memberId;
```

Runtime route parameters remain strings even when a typed match filter changes the argument accepted by `Router.paths`.

## Keep shared layouts mounted

Solid Router reuses a matched route context while its route definition remains in the next match chain.
Moving between sibling child routes therefore keeps their shared parent layout mounted.

Place shared navigation, providers, and route-independent state in the parent component.
Place child-specific state in the leaf component.

The root render prop passed to `<Router>` is outside the matched route chain.
The root render prop stays mounted across all routes.
Use the root render prop for application-wide layout and providers.
Use nested route components for section layouts.

## Load nested data

Every matched route can define a `preload`.
The router calls each preload while it creates the corresponding route context.
The preload return value becomes that route component's `props.data`.

```tsx
const routes = defineRoutes([
	defineRoute({
		path: "/teams/:teamId",
		preload: ({ params }) => getTeam(params.teamId),
		component: (props) => (
			<TeamContext.Provider value={props.data}>
				{props.children}
			</TeamContext.Provider>
		),
		children: [
			defineRoute({
				path: "/members/:memberId",
				preload: ({ params }) => getMember(params.memberId),
				component: (props) => <Member member={props.data} />,
			}),
		],
	}),
]);
```

Route preloads are also used by hover and focus preloading and by the optional single-flight server integration.
See [Data loading and mutations](https://v2-rebuild--solid-docs-v2.netlify.app/routing/solid-router/data) for those behaviors.
---

# Navigation and typed paths

Source: https://v2-rebuild--solid-docs-v2.netlify.app/routing/solid-router/navigation.md

Solid Router uses regular anchors for declarative navigation.
Same-origin anchors inside the router base are intercepted through delegated events.

```tsx
<nav>
	<a href={Router.paths()}>Home</a>
	<a href={Router.paths.users(42)}>User</a>
	<a href="https://example.com">External site</a>
</nav>
```

The router does not intercept these anchors:

* External URLs
* Non-HTTP schemes
* Downloads
* Anchors with `rel="external"`
* Anchors with a `target`

The browser handles these anchors.

## Build URLs with `Router.paths`

The router infers the instance's `paths` proxy from the literal route tree.
Property access adds static segments, and calls bind route parameters.

```tsx
Router.paths();
// "/"

Router.paths.users(42).settings();
// "/users/42/settings"

Router.paths.search({ q: "solid", page: 2 }, "results");
// "/search?q=solid&page=2#results"
```

Every path node converts to a string when used as an `href`, navigation target, or redirect target.
Call a node with no arguments when an API requires a plain `string`.

`matchFilters` can narrow path-builder inputs.
For example, `{ id: int }` makes `paths.users(id)` accept a number while the route component receives `params.id` as a string.

A route `search` schema changes the search object accepted by the corresponding path end.
See [Typed search parameters](#type-search-parameters) for parsed reads.

## Navigate from code

`useNavigate` returns the navigator for the current router session.

```tsx
import { useNavigate } from "@solidjs/router";
import { Router } from "../router";

function SaveButton() {
	const navigate = useNavigate();

	return (
		<button onClick={() => navigate(Router.paths.account, { replace: true })}>
			Open account
		</button>
	);
}
```

Navigation options control:

* Path resolution
* History replacement
* Scrolling
* Location state

Relative strings resolve as URLs against the current location.
Leading `/` strings resolve under the router base.
Pass a number to move through history, such as `navigate(-1)`.

Use an anchor when navigation is represented by a link in the interface.
Use `useNavigate` for navigation caused by application logic.

## Read location and parameters

`useLocation` returns the reactive location object:

```tsx
const location = useLocation();

location.pathname;
location.search;
location.hash;
location.query;
location.state;
location.key;
```

`useParams` returns the merged parameters for the current route chain:

```tsx
const params = useParams();
params.id;
```

Pass a typed path node to narrow the known parameter keys:

```tsx
const params = useParams(Router.paths.users);
params.id;
```

## Type search parameters

Without a schema, `useSearchParams()` reads raw string or string-array values.
The `setSearchParams` function merges values into the current query string.
By default, the update navigates without scrolling.
Empty strings, `undefined`, and `null` remove a key.

```tsx
const [search, setSearch] = useSearchParams();

<button onClick={() => setSearch({ page: Number(search.page || 0) + 1 })}>
	Next page
</button>;
```

Pass a path node whose route defines a synchronous Standard Schema validator to parse and type search values:

```tsx
const [search, setSearch] = useSearchParams(Router.paths.search);

search.page;
setSearch({ page: search.page + 1 });
```

The router runs schemas from the current root-to-leaf match chain.
Successful schema outputs merge over the raw query values.
A schema result with issues is skipped.
An asynchronous schema result throws because asynchronous search validation is not supported.

## Style active and pending links

Claimed route anchors receive state attributes:

* `aria-current="page"` marks an exact match.
* `data-active` marks an exact or descendant match.
* `data-pending` marks the target of an in-flight navigation.

The root path matches exactly for active state.

```css
nav a[aria-current="page"] {
	font-weight: 600;
}

nav a[data-active] {
	color: var(--accent);
}

a[data-pending] {
	opacity: 0.6;
}
```

Use `useLinkState` when a custom link component needs reactive `active`, `current`, or `pending` accessors.

```tsx
function TabLink(props: { href: string; children: JSX.Element }) {
	const state = useLinkState(() => props.href, { end: true });

	return (
		<a href={props.href} data-selected={state.current() || undefined}>
			{props.children}
		</a>
	);
}
```

Configure anchor navigation with these attributes:

* `replace` replaces the current history entry.
* `noscroll` prevents scrolling after navigation.
* `state` supplies JSON for `location.state`.
* `preload` controls route-data preloading.

Set `preload="false"` to keep route code preloading while skipping that link's route-data preload.
Set `link` when the router uses `explicitLinks: true`.

## Observe and guard navigation

`useIsRouting()` returns an accessor that is true while a programmatic or native navigation waits for its route work to settle.
`useMatch()` tests a supplied pattern against the current pathname.
`useRouteMatches()` returns the resolved route-definition match chain.

```tsx
const section = useMatch(() => "/docs/*rest");
const matches = useRouteMatches();
const isRouting = useIsRouting();
```

Use `useBeforeLeave` to prevent a route change and retry it after confirmation:

```tsx
useBeforeLeave((event) => {
	if (!dirty()) return;

	event.preventDefault();

	if (window.confirm("Discard unsaved changes?")) {
		event.retry(true);
	}
});
```

The guard applies to router navigation and supported browser history traversal.
Pass `true` to `retry` to skip the leave handlers for the retried navigation.

See the [navigation API reference](https://v2-rebuild--solid-docs-v2.netlify.app/reference/solid-router/navigation) for all primitive signatures.
---

# Data loading and mutations

Source: https://v2-rebuild--solid-docs-v2.netlify.app/routing/solid-router/data.md

Solid Router's data APIs are optional.
Use route preloads to start work for a match.
Use `query` for keyed caching and revalidation.
Use `action` for URL-addressable mutations and submission records.

## Start work in a route preload

A route `preload` runs before its component is created.
The preload receives the matched parameters, target location, and an intent that identifies why the work started.

```tsx
const userRoute = defineRoute({
	path: "/users/:id",
	preload: ({ params }) => getUser(params.id),
	component: (props) => <User user={props.data} />,
});
```

The preload return value becomes `props.data`.
The intent is one of:

* `"initial"` for the first render or server render.
* `"navigate"` for router navigation.
* `"native"` for browser history traversal.
* `"preload"` for route warming without navigation.

The factory-level `preload` runs for the root layout with parameters merged from the full match chain.
The factory-level preload result becomes the root render prop's `props.data`.

## Preload from links

By default, these link interactions warm matched lazy components and route data:

* Hover
* Focus
* Touch

The router begins hover preloading after a short delay and deduplicates later query reads through the preload cache.

Disable route-data preloading for one link with `preload="false"`:

```tsx
<a href={Router.paths.reports} preload="false">
	Reports
</a>
```

Set `preloadLinks: false` in the router config to disable automatic link preloading.
Use `usePreloadRoute` for an explicit warm-up:

```tsx
const preloadRoute = usePreloadRoute();

preloadRoute(Router.paths.users(42), { preloadData: true });
```

The route's lazy components always warm during explicit preloading.
Set `preloadData: true` to run its preloads.

## Cache reads with `query`

Wrap a read function with `query` and give it a stable key name:

```tsx
import { query } from "@solidjs/router";

export const getUser = query(async (id: string) => {
	const response = await fetch(`/api/users/${id}`);
	return response.json();
}, "users");
```

`query` serializes the arguments after the name to form each cache key.
The wrapped function exposes two keys:

* `.key` is the broad query key.
* `.keyFor(...)` is the key for specific arguments.

```ts
getUser.key;
// "users"

getUser.keyFor("42");
// 'users["42"]'
```

The cache is request-scoped on the server and application-scoped in the browser.
In the browser, a preload result is reused for five seconds so a preload and route render share work.
Tracked consumers keep their cache entry live.
Native back and forward navigation can reuse retained entries.
Unused browser entries older than three minutes are removed by the periodic cache sweep.

Read the query through a Solid async-aware primitive:

```tsx
import { createMemo } from "solid-js";

const user = createMemo(() => getUser(params.id));

return <h1>{user().name}</h1>;
```

When `query` wraps an undeclared server function, `query` declares the reference as a GET operation through the Solid server-function transport.
`query` calls plain functions without that transport behavior.

## Revalidate cached reads

Call `revalidate` with a broad query key or an argument-specific key:

```tsx
revalidate(getUser.key);
revalidate(getUser.keyFor("42"));
```

Omit the key to revalidate every client cache entry.
Keys use prefix matching, so a broad key reaches every argument combination for that query.
The default forced revalidation marks matching entries stale and retriggers live consumers.

## Define an action

`action` wraps an asynchronous mutation.
The returned action has a URL.
When the input shape supports form submission, serialize the action into a form's `action` or `formaction` attribute.

```tsx
import { action } from "@solidjs/router";
import { redirect } from "@solidjs/web";

const updateProfile = action(async (form: FormData) => {
	await saveProfile(form);
	return redirect("/account");
}, "update-profile");
```

Submit actions with POST:

```tsx
<form action={updateProfile} method="post">
	<label>
		Display name
		<input name="displayName" />
	</label>
	<button>Save</button>
</form>
```

The delegated action pipeline marks the submitting form with `aria-busy="true"` until the action and its revalidation settle.
Only POST forms are supported for actions.

Give a server-rendered client action a stable explicit name.
The name lets the server and client serialize the same action URL.
A server function already supplies its server-action URL.

## Bind action arguments

Use `.with(...)` to bind typed arguments ahead of form input:

```tsx
const deleteTodo = action(async (id: string, form: FormData) => {
	await removeTodo(id);
});

<form action={deleteTodo.with(todo.id)} method="post">
	<button>Delete</button>
</form>;
```

`.with(...)` encodes the bound arguments in the action URL.

## Add optimistic and settled behavior

`.onSubmit` runs owner-scoped callbacks before the mutation settles.
`.onSettled` receives a `Submission` after every completion, including void and redirect outcomes.
Solid removes listeners registered under a reactive owner when the owner is disposed.

```tsx
const addTodo = action(async (todo: TodoInput) => {
	await saveTodo(todo);
	return todo;
}, "add-todo").onSubmit((todo) => {
	setTodos((items) => {
		items.push({ ...todo, pending: true });
	});
});
```

Use Solid's optimistic primitives to hold in-flight rendered state.
Use `useSubmissions` for settled action results and errors.

```tsx
const submissions = useSubmissions(addTodo);
const latest = () => submissions.at(-1);

latest()?.result;
latest()?.error;
latest()?.clear();
latest()?.retry();
```

The router retains only outcomes with a result or an error in the submissions list.
A void or metadata-only completion still reaches `.onSettled`.

## Invoke an action from code

`useAction` binds an action to the current router context:

```tsx
const submit = useAction(updateSettings);

await submit(settings());
```

Direct invocation requires client-side JavaScript and does not provide a browser form fallback.

## Revalidate after a mutation

Response metadata from `@solidjs/web` can carry revalidation keys and redirects.
The router invalidates matching query entries and performs relative redirects through router navigation.

With the router's single-flight server-function integration, a mutation response can contain fresh query values from the destination route's preloads.
The client seeds those values before the action call resolves.
See [Server rendering and hydration](https://v2-rebuild--solid-docs-v2.netlify.app/routing/solid-router/server-rendering#collect-fresh-data-with-mutations) for the server setup.

See the [data API reference](https://v2-rebuild--solid-docs-v2.netlify.app/reference/solid-router/data) for signatures and cache controls.
---

# Server rendering and hydration

Source: https://v2-rebuild--solid-docs-v2.netlify.app/routing/solid-router/server-rendering.md

The same router instance renders on the client and server.
No separate server router is required.

## Select the server URL

When a request event is available, the router reads `event.request.url`.
For static generation, tests, or another render without a request event, pass a `url` prop:

```tsx
const html = await renderToStream(() => (
	<Router url="https://example.com/users/42" />
));
```

A request event takes precedence over the `url` prop.
Only the pathname and search string select the server route.
Client history adapters do not select the location for a server render, although their utility overrides remain available.

## Stream lazy route subtrees

A matched lazy `children` thunk is asynchronous route work.
The router waits for the subtree, recompiles the route branches, and then renders its inner match.

Use the streaming server-rendering entry for a route tree that can cross a lazy subtree:

```tsx
import { renderToStream } from "@solidjs/web";

const html = await renderToStream(() => <Router url={request.url} />);
```

Await `renderToStream` to receive the settled HTML.
The synchronous `renderToString` path does not support a matched lazy route subtree.

## Hydrate query results

During an asynchronous server render, `query` serializes its keyed result into Solid's hydration registry unless the render disables hydration or the request is marked server-only.
The client query with the same name and serialized arguments adopts that server result instead of starting a duplicate request.

The adoption path remains available after global hydration completes because a lazy route module can make its first matching query read later.
The server payload must be younger than the query cache retention limit of three minutes.
A first read after that limit runs the query again instead of presenting the old server value as newly fetched.

Keep the query name and arguments stable between server and client.
The cache key is the query name followed by the serialized argument array.

## Collect fresh data with mutations

The optional `@solidjs/router/server` entry connects route preloads to Solid's server-function single-flight protocol.
Create the collector from the same router instance:

```ts
// src/server-config.ts
import { createFlightDataCollector } from "@solidjs/router/server";
import { configureServerFunctionsServer } from "@solidjs/web/server-functions/server";
import { Router } from "./router";

configureServerFunctionsServer({
	collectFlightData: createFlightDataCollector(Router),
});
```

The collector receives the mutation outcome from the server-function handler.
The collector then:

1. Matches the URL that the client will show after the mutation.
2. Resolves matching lazy route subtrees.
3. Runs the root preload.
4. Runs the matched route preloads in data-only mode.

When those preloads call `query`, the mutation response includes the keyed values.

On the client, a mounted router subscribes to flight data when `singleFlight` is enabled.
The subscription opts server-function mutations into the protocol.
The router seeds delivered values into the query cache.
The router then applies response revalidation and redirect metadata before the action caller receives the return value.

Set `singleFlight: false` to keep the router from subscribing.
Without a consumer, the transport does not add the single-flight request header and the server does not run collection for that mutation.

## Register the collector before dispatch

When `@solidjs/vite-plugin` compiles server functions, its `serverFunctions.configure` option can pin a server-only configuration module into the generated handler graph:

```ts
// vite.config.ts
import solid from "@solidjs/vite-plugin";

export default {
	plugins: [
		solid({
			serverFunctions: {
				configure: "./src/server-config.ts",
			},
		}),
	],
};
```

The generated handler imports the configuration module before it configures and dispatches server-function requests.
The import makes the collector available for the first mutation in development and production.
The default server-function endpoint is `/_server`; keep the router's `actionBase` aligned with a customized endpoint.

Applications with a custom server-function handler can instead pass `collectFlightData` to `handleServerFunctionRequest` for each request.

## Preserve form behavior without JavaScript

An action backed by a server function has a URL that a POST form can submit without the client runtime.
The Solid server-function runtime handles that no-JavaScript post by redirecting back with the outcome in a one-shot flash cookie.
During the redirected server render, the router clears and decodes that cookie into submission state.

The router also intercepts server-action form URLs in clients where the action module was not loaded.
The router loads the action submit path on demand.
The router then sends the form through the server-function transport.

The no-JavaScript form path requires the Solid server-function handler.
Plain client-only actions cannot run without their client module.

See [`createFlightDataCollector`](https://v2-rebuild--solid-docs-v2.netlify.app/reference/solid-router/server#createflightdatacollector) for the collector options.
---

# TanStack Router

Source: https://v2-rebuild--solid-docs-v2.netlify.app/routing/tanstack.md

The `fullstack-tanstack` template pairs [TanStack Router](https://tanstack.com/router) with [TanStack Query](https://tanstack.com/query) in `@solidjs/vite-plugin` start mode.
TanStack Router owns route matching and navigation, while TanStack Query owns the application data cache.
The Solid plugin provides the generated entries, document stream, request event, and server-function endpoint wiring.
The `@solidjs/web` server-function runtime owns the single-flight envelope and exposes hooks for the application's data strategy.

Use the [TanStack Router documentation](https://tanstack.com/router/latest/docs/framework/solid/overview) for route definitions, navigation, loaders, search parameters, and other router APIs.
This page documents the integration boundary used by the template.

## Route setup

`@tanstack/router-plugin` scans `src/routes` and generates `src/routeTree.gen.ts`.
The TanStack plugin is registered before `@solidjs/vite-plugin` in `vite.config.ts`.

The application creates each router through a shared `createAppRouter(queryClient, history?)` factory.
The factory passes the generated route tree and the `QueryClient` through the router context.
Route loaders use that context to prefetch query options, and route components read the same options with `useQuery`.

The template configures intent preloading and disables TanStack Router's global catch boundary.
With that option enabled, TanStack Router's `Matches` component omits its internal `Loading` wrapper and catch boundary.
The template source comments describe matching the server and client trees as the reason for this setting.

The template reserves `src/routes` for TanStack Router.
Its `filesystem-routing` plugin scans `src/api` instead, maps those files under `/api`, and exposes their handler manifest through `virtual:file-routes`.
The middleware consumes that manifest to dispatch API requests.

## Server-side rendering ownership

The Solid plugin owns the HTML stream and renders the configured `Document` around the prepared application.
The template does not use TanStack Router's `RouterServer`, `RouterClient`, or stream bootstrap protocol.
It uses TanStack Router for matching and loading, then uses TanStack Query's public dehydration APIs for the data handoff.

`start.setup` points to `src/setup.tsx` and runs each time the generated server entry renders an SSR request.
The setup function creates a request-local `QueryClient` and a TanStack router with memory history initialized from the request pathname and search string.
It awaits `router.load()`, waits for the queries started by matched loaders to settle, and stores `dehydrate(queryClient)` on `event.locals`.
It then returns a `QueryClientProvider` and `RouterProvider` tree for the generated server entry to render in place of `App`.

`src/Document.tsx` serializes the dehydrated state into `window.__QUERY_STATE__`.
The serialization escapes `<` before placing the JSON in an inline script.
On the client, `src/App.tsx` creates one module-scoped `QueryClient` and hydrates it from that state.
Module evaluation then awaits `router.load()` before the exported `App` component can render its providers.

## Query ownership and hydration

Each read in the template is declared as TanStack `queryOptions` around a Solid server function.
Matched route loaders start `queryClient.prefetchQuery(...)` without returning its promise.
The setup function therefore waits for pending query-cache promises after `router.load()` before dehydrating the cache.
Components call `useQuery` with the same query options.

The shared `QueryClient` factory sets `staleTime` to 30,000 milliseconds.

## Single-flight mutations

The integration uses a dehydrated `QueryClient` as its single-flight payload.
The payload crosses only public extension points provided by the server-function runtime and TanStack Query.

On the server, `serverFunctions.configure` imports `src/server-config.ts` before server-function dispatch.
Its `collectFlightData` hook receives the target URL and headers containing the mutation's folded cookie effects.
The hook creates a fresh TanStack router and `QueryClient` for that URL, loads the matched routes, and waits for their queries.
It returns `dehydrate(queryClient)` when the resulting state contains queries and returns no flight payload otherwise.

On the client, `subscribeFlightData` opts non-GET server-function calls into collection while its consumer is registered.
Its consumer passes each payload to TanStack Query's `hydrate(queryClient, data)`.
The server-function transport awaits that consumer before resolving the mutation call.
The template's mutation options do not call `invalidateQueries` after the mutation.

Mutations remain Solid server functions invoked through TanStack Query's `useMutation`.
The template wraps each server-function call in its own `mutationFn`.
